# Implementation Status – AI_OS

**Project:** AI_OS (AI Operating System)
**Document:** Implementation Status
**Version:** 1.0
**Status:** Active
**Priority reprioritized (2026-07-27, product owner):** deepening Retrieval/LLM Gateway/Security is now paused. The stated priority is the shortest real path to a working multi-agent software-engineering pipeline — Architecture Agent proposes a design → multiple Build Agents implement it → Test Agents verify it → a Documentation Agent records it for future LLM context. Nothing already built is removed; this is a reprioritization of what gets built next, not a rollback. See this entry's own step below for the first increment under the new priority.

**Last Updated:** 2026-07-28 (a documentation-reconciliation and verification-debt-closing step — not a new increment; the health audit's own findings are now closed, and Docker Desktop genuinely became available partway through this step for the first time in this project's history. **Renamed** the agent-catalog naming fork the audit found: `architect` → `architecture` and `test` → `qa-test` (matching `agents.md`'s own Approved catalog exactly), `build` kept and formally added to that catalog as a new entry (no existing documented id fit its real, generic scope), and the workflow kept as `se.delivery_pipeline` with its own new, honest entry in `workflows.md` rather than force-renamed into the incompatible `se.implement_task` shape. Closed every doc-drift gap the audit named: `context_manager.md`/`workflow_architecture.md` now record `WorkflowStepOutputResolver`; `capability_pack_contract.md` carries a dated exception note for the pack's real direct-Kernel-import compromise; `manifest_schema.md` and the JSON schema itself were fixed so `modelAlias` is required only when an agent declares `llm:invoke` (the QA/Test Agent's own manifest entry no longer needs a decoy value); `llm_gateway.md` §15's dead `platform_sdk/contracts/llm.py` reference is corrected; ADR-0016/`security_architecture.md` now cross-reference the real `SandboxExecutor` name against `SandboxRuntime`. Fixed all ~30 Postgres/testcontainers fixtures (via one new shared helper, `tests/integration/_postgres_fixture.py`) to skip cleanly, with a clear reason, instead of raising a raw `docker.errors.DockerException` — verified directly both ways (a real clean skip with Docker forced unavailable, and a real, full, passing run with it available). **Docker Desktop became available mid-step** — the single highest-priority re-verification item in this entire document for the last several increments — and the resulting real run found and fixed two genuine bugs in `DockerSandbox` (missing automatic image pull; and a real Windows/npipe-specific bug where `detach=True` silently prevented stdin EOF from ever reaching a container, hanging until timeout — fixed by using `detach=False`, which has no effect on this class's own control flow). With both fixed, **all 7 real, unmocked `DockerSandbox` live guarantee tests pass**, and a **full real run of the entire integration suite** (not a manual trace, not `--collect-only`) reports **218 passed, 10 correctly-skipped (no live API key), 0 failed** — including the delivery pipeline's own deterministic full-chain test, for real, for the first time. See §3 for the full reasoning)

**Previously:** 2026-07-28 (eighth increment under the new priority — a real ADR-0016 Tier 1 sandbox backend, `ai_os_kernel.sandbox.docker_executor.DockerSandbox`, implementing the existing `SandboxExecutor` Protocol unchanged: an ephemeral OCI container per call, network disabled, a read-only root filesystem with exactly one bind-mounted writable working directory, dropped Linux capabilities, `no-new-privileges`, a non-root user, and real memory/CPU/PID limits — a materially fuller `SandboxGuarantees` matrix (5/5 `True`) than `LocalSubprocessSandbox`'s own honest 3/5)

**Before that:** 2026-07-28 (seventh increment, the first real, complete, demonstrable product loop — Architecture → Build → Test → Documentation chained via `WorkflowStepOutputResolver`; sixth increment — a Documentation Agent; fifth increment — a Test Agent with **no LLM call at all**; fourth increment — a Build Agent; third increment — the first real, thin Capability Pack with the Architecture Agent; second and first increments — a real, minimal `SandboxedCommandTool`/narrowed `ToolStepExecutor` guard, and a minimal Tier 1 Sandbox Executor; and, before the reprioritization, Retrieval's three real increments — see §3 for the fuller history)

---

## 1. Purpose

A living record of what has actually been built, checked against the exit
criteria in `implementation_roadmap.md`, so any session (human or AI) can
resume work without rescanning the repository or relying on chat history.

This document is descriptive, not authoritative — on any conflict, the
Implementation Roadmap and the ADRs govern.

---

## 2. Current Stage

**Stage A – Platform Skeleton** is process-complete but not exit-criteria-complete
(see §4). **Stage B – Minimum Viable Kernel** is underway: the persistence
foundation, definition loading/validation, instance creation, the first state
transition (`created` → `running`), full step-by-step progression through to
completion, real (trivial) Agent and Tool invocation paths, the full lease
acquire → renew → release cycle, a reusable single-shot claim-and-advance
composition, a bounded run-to-completion loop for a single instance, a
background lease expiry reaper, a materialised `workflow_steps` row written
alongside every executed step with a read accessor (`list_steps`) for that
history, the `approvals` table schema (data_model.md §4.5 — still without a
writer), and a read accessor (`list_events`) for the append-only event log are
built and verified. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` is now aligned with the
repository as it actually stands (see §3) and would run green, though it has
never actually executed on GitHub's servers (see §5). A minimal Secrets
Resolution component (`SecretProvider` Protocol, `EnvSecretProvider` — the
`env` backend only) now exists per ADR-0024, not yet wired into any consumer.
`TraceIdMiddleware` now creates a real OpenTelemetry span per request,
exported via the OTel console exporter, with every log line emitted while a
span is active carrying that span's own `trace_id`/`span_id`, and now also
increments a real `aios.http.requests` counter per request, exported via the
OTel console metric exporter (ADR-0017). The `governance` schema (data_model.md
§9 — ADR-0017's separate tamper-evident, hash-chained path) is now complete:
`audit_log` and `config_changes`, both schema and migration only, still
without a writer. Two of the three `platform` schema (§10) tables —
`event_outbox` (ADR-0012's transactional outbox) and `idempotency_keys` —
now exist the same way; the third, `platform.schema_metadata`, is
deliberately deferred (see §4) since data_model.md gives it no column list
to mirror. The `trace` schema (§8) is now complete too — `artifacts` and
`links`, schema and migration only, still without a writer, with `links`
foreign-keyed to `artifacts` and its documented partial-unique open-link
rule enforced. `catalog.workflow_definitions`, `catalog.prompts`, `catalog.tools`, and
`catalog.agents` (data_model.md §5 — every *cleanly specified* `catalog`
table, 4 of 6; `packs`/`pack_state_transitions` were blocked on a
missing-primary-key gap, see §4 — `packs`' own primary key (`pack_id`) is
now resolved, `pack_state_transitions`' is not) exist the same way;
`workflow_definitions` unblocks — as a distinct, later step, the
`definition_id` versioning question that was previously open is now also
resolved (see below) — the foreign key `persistence/schema.py` has
deferred from `workflow_instances`/`workflow_events` since the first
migration. `evaluation.run_manifests`
(data_model.md §6 — 1 of 6 `evaluation` tables) now exists too, and its own
`workflow_id` column is the first cross-schema foreign key in this codebase
to actually reference a table that already exists (`workflow_instances`),
rather than deferring. The reverse foreign key — `workflow_instances.
run_manifest_id` → `evaluation.run_manifests.run_manifest_id`, deferred
since the very first migration — is now retrofitted too, via a new
`persistence/cross_schema_foreign_keys.py` module rather than an inline
`sa.ForeignKey(...)` in `schema.py` itself, since the two schema modules
already import each other's tables in the opposite direction and a
second, reverse import would be a genuine circular import.
`evaluation.experiment_runs` and now `evaluation.experiments` exist too
(3 of 6 `evaluation` tables), with `experiment_runs.experiment_id`
retrofitted to a real foreign key against `experiments.experiment_id` in
the same step that added `experiments` — a same-module, same-`MetaData`
retrofit, trivial compared to the earlier cross-module
`workflow_instances.run_manifest_id` one. `experiments.definition_id`/
`definition_version` deliberately got no foreign key when added,
consistent with the `definition_id` versioning ambiguity that was still
open at the time (now resolved — see below). `evaluation.gate_results`
now exists too (4 of 6 `evaluation` tables), chosen deliberately over
`llm_calls` (more inferential judgment calls) and `metrics` (still
blocked on undocumented `numeric` precision). A documentation-only step
has since resolved every structural ambiguity that was standing in the
way of the remaining schema work — `data_model.md` now records: the
`definition_id`/`definition_version` composite-key resolution (`workflow_
instances`' pair forms a composite FK against `catalog.workflow_
definitions`' new composite PK, `(definition_id, version)`); `catalog.
packs`' primary key (`pack_id`); `evaluation.metrics.metric_value`'s
precision (`NUMERIC(20,6)`); and `evaluation.llm_calls.cost_usd`'s
precision (`NUMERIC(18,6)`) plus real foreign keys on `agent_id`/
`prompt_id`. None of this has been implemented yet — `catalog.
workflow_definitions` still has its original single-column PK in the
database, and at the time `catalog.packs`/`pack_state_transitions` and
`evaluation.metrics`/`llm_calls` still didn't exist — the documentation
step only specified what to build. `catalog.
pack_state_transitions`' own primary key remains undecided; that gap is
untouched. `catalog.packs` now exists too (5 of 6 `catalog` tables) —
`pack_id` PK, an eight-value `state` `CHECK` constraint grounded in
`capability_manager.md` §4's canonical lifecycle, and `installed_at`/
`activated_at`/`health` genuinely nullable (a freshly-discovered pack
has not reached those states yet). `pack_state_transitions` remains the
only unbuilt `catalog` table, still blocked on its own undocumented
primary key; `workflow_definitions`/`prompts`/`tools`/`agents`' own
`pack_id` columns remain un-FK'd to `packs.pack_id` — technically
unblocked now, but a distinct, later step, not this one. `evaluation.
metrics` now exists too (5 of 6 `evaluation` tables) — `metric_value`
typed `NUMERIC(20,6)` per the now-resolved documentation decision,
`recorded_at` application-supplied (no server default, mirroring
`workflow_events.occurred_at`/`audit_log.occurred_at`), `run_id`
indexed but deliberately not yet FK'd to `experiment_runs.run_id` (out
of scope for that step). `evaluation.llm_calls` now exists too —
`cost_usd` typed `NUMERIC(18,6)`, `agent_id`/`prompt_id` real-FK'd to
`catalog.agents`/`catalog.prompts` (the first `evaluation`-schema
foreign keys pointing at `catalog`, not `workflow`), completing the
`evaluation` schema at 6 of 6 tables. `catalog.workflow_definitions` has
since been migrated to the documented composite primary key
`(definition_id, version)`, resolving the versioning ambiguity flagged
since the first `catalog` step. The paired foreign-key retrofit on
`workflow_instances` was attempted, then deliberately reverted after
verifying — not assuming — that it breaks 37 of 39 Workflow Engine
integration tests: nothing writes to `catalog.workflow_definitions` yet,
while `SqlWorkflowInstanceRepository.create()` already writes
`workflow_instances` rows unconditionally, with no matching catalog row
to reference. That FK stays deferred, now for a newly-discovered,
different reason than the original ambiguity (see §3). `catalog.
pack_state_transitions` now exists too, completing the `catalog` schema
at 6 of 6 tables: its primary key (`transition_id`) and required
foreign key (`pack_id` → `packs.pack_id`) were an approved decision
recorded in data_model.md §5 in the same step that implements them,
mirroring exactly how `packs`' own primary key was decided before that
table was built. The previously-deferred `workflow_instances` →
`catalog.workflow_definitions` foreign key is now genuinely enforced:
a new, deliberately minimal
`ai_os_kernel.workflow_engine.definition_catalog.SqlWorkflowDefinitionCatalog`
registers a validated `WorkflowDefinition` (an idempotent upsert keyed
on `(definition_id, version)`) the moment before
`WorkflowInstanceService.create_instance()` writes the instance that
references it — every call, not conditionally — which is what makes
the foreign key safe this time where it previously was not. This is
not a Capability Manager and not a Manifest Loader extension; it is the
smallest seam that closes the gap the earlier revert identified. The
**LLM Gateway** now exists too, as a deliberately minimal first slice:
an `LLMGateway` `Protocol` (`complete(request) -> response`), a reduced
`LLMRequest`/`LLMResponse` shape (roughly a quarter of `llm_gateway.md`
§4/§5's full documented contract — no tool-calling, structured output,
thinking/effort, streaming, or caching fields), and `EchoLLMGateway`,
the one trivial in-process implementation, mirroring `EchoAgent`/
`EchoTool` exactly. One of §3's fourteen subsystems now has a minimal
slice too — Observability: `SqlLLMCallRecorder` writes one
`evaluation.llm_calls` row after a completed `EchoLLMGateway.complete()`
call, with every value honestly sourced from the real
`LLMRequest`/`LLMResponse`/`UsageRecord` already produced (no invented
data). It is a standalone writer, not a wrapper around `LLMGateway`
itself — composed explicitly by a caller, since the reduced
`LLMRequest` contract does not carry the workflow/step/agent/prompt
correlation a decorator would need. `agent_id`/`prompt_id`/
`prompt_version` are optional on the call path but the schema requires
all three together (real `NOT NULL` foreign keys to `catalog.agents`/
`catalog.prompts`, built two steps ago) — providing fewer than three
fails clearly before any database access. Routing, real provider
adapters, budgets, capability negotiation, prompt caching, streaming,
and retry/fallback/rate limiting — the other thirteen subsystems —
still do not exist. The **Prompt Engine** now exists too, as a
deliberately minimal first slice: a `PromptEngine` `Protocol`
(`render(request) -> response`), a reduced `PromptRenderRequest`/
`PromptRenderResponse` shape modelling the runtime call envelope of
`prompt_engine.md` §7's Resolution Flow (not the persisted §6 Prompt
Contract, which needs real `catalog.prompts` loading — deferred), and
`InMemoryPromptEngine`, the one trivial in-process implementation:
templates are supplied directly at construction time (no
`catalog.prompts` reader), rendering does plain `{{variable}}`
substitution (the "simple conditionals" half of §12's restricted
templating syntax is also deferred), and a missing required variable
fails clearly before any substitution is attempted. It never calls the
LLM Gateway itself — `system_architecture.md`'s "LLM Abstraction Path"
is explicit that the Prompt Engine returns a rendered prompt to the
caller rather than proxying the call, mirroring the same
"caller composes explicitly" shape already established for
`LLMCallRecorder`. A new top-level module, `ai_os_kernel.prompted_completion`
(`PromptedCompletionService`), now composes the two: it calls
`PromptEngine.render()`, builds an `LLMRequest` from the rendered
content as the sole user message, calls `LLMGateway.complete()`, and
optionally records the call via the existing `LLMCallRecorder` when a
caller supplies `workflow_id`/`step_id` for that specific call. It is
deliberately not a new architectural subsystem, not a `Protocol` (a
composition of two already-injected Protocols needs no seam of its
own), and not a real `Agent` — nothing in the Workflow Engine calls it
yet, matching `system_architecture.md`'s own framing that an Agent (in
a Capability Pack) is the caller, and no real Agent runtime exists to
be that caller yet. The Prompt Engine now has a second `PromptEngine`
implementation too — `SqlPromptCatalog` (`ai_os_kernel.prompt_engine.
catalog`) reads a template row from `catalog.prompts` by `(prompt_id,
version)` instead of an in-process map, sharing the identical
`render_template()` substitution routine `InMemoryPromptEngine` already
used (extracted into `prompt_engine.renderer` so the two implementations
can only ever differ in where a template comes from). Building this
surfaced a real, previously-undiscovered schema gap: `catalog.prompts`
had `prompt_id` alone as its primary key (data_model.md §5), the same
single-column-PK shape `catalog.workflow_definitions` had before its
own composite-key migration — so two versions of the same `prompt_id`
could not coexist as separate rows. That gap is now closed: migration
`0024_catalog_prompts_pk` moves `catalog.prompts` to the composite
primary key `(prompt_id, version)`, and retrofits
`evaluation.llm_calls`' `prompt_id`/`prompt_version` columns from a
single-column foreign key (against `prompt_id` alone) to a composite
foreign key against that new pair — verified safe against the full test
suite, not assumed, mirroring the `workflow_definitions`/
`workflow_instances` precedent exactly. The two remaining
deliberately-deferred foreign key retrofits are now closed too:
`evaluation.experiments.definition_id`/`definition_version` carries a
composite foreign key against `catalog.workflow_definitions
(definition_id, version)` (migration `0025_experiments_definition_fk`),
and `evaluation.metrics.run_id` carries a foreign key against
`evaluation.experiment_runs.run_id` (migration `0026_metrics_run_id_fk`).
The first was safe against the existing test suite unmodified; the
second was not — three tests referenced a fabricated `run_id` with no
real `experiment_runs` row behind it, discovered by attempting the
retrofit and running the suite rather than assuming, and fixed by
seeding a real row in the two tests that needed one to keep succeeding.
`workflow_architecture.md` documents a **Step Contract**: an `agent`/
`tool` step may declare `agentId`/`toolId` (required for its own step
type) and, for `agent` steps only, optional `promptId`/`promptVersion`/
`modelAlias`. `WorkflowStep` (`ai_os_kernel.workflow_engine.models`)
now validates all five fields against that documented contract exactly
— `agentId` required and `toolId` forbidden on an `agent` step;
`toolId` required and `agentId`/`promptId`/`promptVersion`/`modelAlias`
forbidden on a `tool` step; all five forbidden on every other step
type; `promptId`/`promptVersion` must be declared together or not at
all — mirroring the existing `_parallel_step_requires_join_policy`
validator pattern exactly. This is a **model/validation-only change**:
nothing resolves `agentId`/`toolId` to a real `Agent`/`Tool` instance,
and nothing calls the Prompt Engine or LLM Gateway from a workflow step
— `DispatchingStepExecutor` still invokes the same `EchoAgent`/
`EchoTool` regardless of what a step declares, exactly as before this
step. `agent_architecture.md`'s Invocation lifecycle cross-references
the new fields (`promptId`/`promptVersion`/`modelAlias` inform
`AgentRequest` construction). `SqlWorkflowInstanceRepository.
advance_workflow()` now copies a step's declared `agent_id`/`tool_id`
straight onto the `workflow_steps` row it writes — a writer-only
change; the row records what a step *named*, not what actually ran,
since `DispatchingStepExecutor` still always dispatches to the same
`EchoAgent`/`EchoTool` regardless. `workflow_steps` now also has
`prompt_id`/`prompt_version`/`model_alias` columns (migration
`0027_workflow_steps_prompt`), populated the identical writer-only way
— completing all five of the Step Contract's fields on the executed
step's own persisted record, closing a gap flagged in this document
since before the LLM Gateway existed
(`workflow_steps.agent_id`/`tool_id` were always `NULL`). Deliberately
no foreign key on `prompt_id`/`prompt_version` to `catalog.prompts`:
no writer exists for that table yet, the same reasoning `agent_id`/
`tool_id` were already exempted under. **Real dispatch by declared id
now exists too**: `AgentRegistry`/`ToolRegistry`
(`ai_os_kernel.workflow_engine.registry`) resolve a step's declared
`agentId`/`toolId` to a real, already-constructed `Agent`/`Tool`
instance, and `AgentStepExecutor`/`ToolStepExecutor` now go through an
injected registry instead of always calling the one fixed instance
they used to be constructed with — different steps declaring different
ids now genuinely resolve to different registered instances.
`InMemoryAgentRegistry`/`InMemoryToolRegistry` are the simplest
implementation (a plain mapping the composition root supplies
directly). **A second, `catalog.agents`/`catalog.tools`-backed
implementation now exists too**: `SqlAgentRegistry`/`SqlToolRegistry`
confirm a declared `agentId`/`toolId` is a real, registered catalog
row — not just any string a step happened to type — before returning
the same `EchoAgent`/`EchoTool` stand-in. `SqlToolRegistry` honestly
carries the row's own declared `trust_tier` through to the returned
`EchoTool` (now constructor-settable rather than a fixed class
attribute), so a catalog-declared `tier1_sandboxed` tool is still
refused by `ToolStepExecutor`'s existing ADR-0016 guard even via this
path — no capability is silently laundered into a more-trusted one.
Building this surfaced a real, pre-existing gap: `catalog.agents`/
`catalog.tools` had no `entrypoint` column, even though the Agent/Tool
Contracts have always required one and
`platform_sdk/schemas/manifest.schema.json`'s own `agents[]`/`tools[]`
entries already require and validate one. That gap is now closed too —
migration `0028_catalog_entrypoint` adds a required `entrypoint` column
(`TEXT NOT NULL`, matching the manifest schema's own "Python import
path, `module.path:ClassName`" shape) to both tables, schema and
migration only: nothing reads it yet, and
`SqlAgentRegistry`/`SqlToolRegistry` still return the same `EchoAgent`/
`EchoTool` stand-in regardless of what it says — real entrypoint
loading remains Capability Manager territory (Stage C). **That
entrypoint column now has a reader.** A new, deliberately minimal
`EntrypointLoader` (`ai_os_kernel.workflow_engine.entrypoint_loader`)
resolves an `entrypoint` string of the documented `module.path:
ClassName` form, imports it, and constructs it with no arguments — the
smallest possible mechanism, explicitly not a plugin framework.
`SqlAgentRegistry`/`SqlToolRegistry` now use it: a real catalog row's
declared `entrypoint` is genuinely loaded and constructed, validated
against the now-`@runtime_checkable` `Agent`/`Tool` Protocols via
`isinstance`, with tools additionally checked for `trust_tier`
agreement between what the loaded object's own code declares and what
`catalog.tools` records — a disagreement is rejected outright rather
than either side being silently trusted. A real catalog row can now
resolve to a real, distinguishable implementation, not always
`EchoAgent`/`EchoTool`. **Activation-state gating now exists too — the
first real Capability Manager slice.** `catalog.packs.state` already
carried capability_manager.md §4's canonical eight-value lifecycle
(schema only, since the `catalog.packs` step); `SqlAgentRegistry`/
`SqlToolRegistry` now read it via one `LEFT JOIN` per lookup and refuse
to proceed — before any entrypoint is ever imported — unless a resolved
agent/tool's declared `pack_id` names a real `catalog.packs` row whose
`state` is `activated`, raising the new `PackNotActivatedError`
otherwise (a missing pack row and a real-but-non-`activated` state are
both covered, with distinct messages). A new `PackState` `StrEnum`
(`ai_os_kernel.workflow_engine.pack_state`) mirrors the same eight
values as a proper Python type, the identical role `TrustTier` already
plays for `catalog.tools.trust_tier`. `InMemoryAgentRegistry`/
`InMemoryToolRegistry` are unaffected — there is no pack for a
caller-supplied mapping entry to belong to. Still no pack install/
upgrade lifecycle, no health monitoring, no permissions system, and no
sandboxing — an activated pack owning the declared id is the only
additional thing checked. **The LLM Gateway now has its first real
provider adapter too.** `AnthropicAdapter`
(`ai_os_kernel.llm_gateway.adapters.anthropic_adapter`) calls the real
Anthropic API via `anthropic.AsyncAnthropic` — the only module
permitted to import that SDK (llm_gateway.md §2) — behind the same
`LLMGateway` Protocol `EchoLLMGateway` already implements; `EchoLLMGateway`
remains the deterministic, no-network implementation for tests and
local development, unchanged. `model_alias` resolves to a real model id
and per-model USD pricing through a new, deliberately small
`config/llm.yaml` and `load_provider_config()` — a flat mapping, not the
documented Router's alias chains/fallback/provider health/experiment
pinning — and the adapter's own API key is resolved through the
existing `SecretProvider` (`build_anthropic_adapter()`), never read from
`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` directly. Every non-2xx response and every network
failure is wrapped into one new `LLMProviderError`; a real safety
refusal (`stop_reason: "refusal"`) is `LLMRefusalError`, a distinct,
real outcome rather than a failure. No routing mesh, no budget system,
no streaming, no multi-provider fallback matrix, no capability
negotiation, and no prompt caching — those remain the Gateway's
thirteen other documented internal subsystems, still out of scope.
Nothing in the Workflow Engine calls the Gateway yet, so this adapter is
not wired into the composition root — the same "exists and is tested,
not yet consumed" position `EchoLLMGateway` itself started from.
**That composition now has a real, tested, one-call assembly point.**
`build_anthropic_prompted_completion_service()`
(`ai_os_kernel.prompted_completion`) composes a real `SqlPromptCatalog`,
a real `AnthropicAdapter` (its key resolved via `SecretProvider`, same
as before), and a real `SqlLLMCallRecorder` into one
`PromptedCompletionService` — the exact "Agent / caller -> PromptEngine
-> LLMGateway(AnthropicAdapter) -> optional llm_calls recording" chain
system_architecture.md's LLM Abstraction Path documents, now provable
end to end: a real `catalog.prompts` row renders, a real Anthropic call
completes it, and — when a caller supplies `workflow_id`/`step_id` —a
real `evaluation.llm_calls` row is written, all verified by a new opt-in
live test making one real, cheap call. `PromptedCompletionService`
itself is unchanged; `EchoLLMGateway`/`InMemoryPromptEngine` remain the
deterministic path its own existing unit tests already used and still
use. Like `AnthropicAdapter` before it, this factory is not wired into
`kernel/bootstrap.py` or any real API surface. **The Workflow Engine
now has a real caller for it, though.** `PromptedAgent`
(`ai_os_kernel.workflow_engine.prompted_agent`) is the first real,
non-`Echo*` `Agent`: it reads `promptId`/`promptVersion`/`modelAlias`
from its `inputs` and delegates to an injected
`PromptedCompletionService`, returning the completion's text as
`{"content": ...}` — exactly the invocation lifecycle
agent_architecture.md already documented ("those are inputs to the
agent's own invocation ... the Workflow Engine passes them through
without acting on them itself"). `AgentStepExecutor` now does that
passing-through: it forwards a step's declared `promptId`/
`promptVersion`/`modelAlias` as `inputs` instead of always invoking with
`{}` — a step declaring none of them still executes with an empty
`inputs` dict, unchanged from before, and `EchoAgent` still ignores
whatever it receives either way. `PromptedAgent` is constructor-injected
and used through `InMemoryAgentRegistry`, not `SqlAgentRegistry`'s
zero-argument entrypoint loading — passing real configuration into a
dynamically-loaded entrypoint's constructor is explicitly Capability
Manager territory the entrypoint loader's own docstring already fences
off, not something this step reopens. A new opt-in live test proves the
complete chain for the first time: a real `WorkflowStep` executed by the
real `AgentStepExecutor`, through a real `AgentRegistry`, resolving to
`PromptedAgent`, calling the real Anthropic API. **The real composition
root now builds and registers this agent too, not just tests.**
`kernel/bootstrap.py` gained a `_lifespan` hook — FastAPI's own
async-startup mechanism, since `build_app()` itself runs synchronously
at import time, before any event loop exists, and secret resolution is
async. On real app startup (verified, not assumed, to be genuinely
distinct from a bare `TestClient(app)`, which never triggers it), this
builds the identical real chain — `DatabaseSettings` → `build_engine` →
`load_provider_config` → `build_anthropic_prompted_completion_service`
→ `PromptedAgent` — and attaches an `InMemoryAgentRegistry` holding it
under `platform/prompted-agent` as `app.state.agent_registry`. A
missing or misconfigured database/secret degrades to an **empty**
registry with a logged warning rather than preventing the Kernel from
starting — Stage A functionality (health checks) must not depend on a
Stage B integration being configured, the identical reasoning already
applied to `manifest_loader_check`'s own try/except. Nothing yet reads
`app.state.agent_registry` from a real HTTP route or a real worker
loop — the Workflow Engine itself is still not wired into
`kernel/bootstrap.py` at all — so this closes "construct it for real"
without yet closing "a real request triggers it," the still-open "No
API surface" gap (§4). The Context Manager, real per-step input mapping
beyond these three fields, the Human Approval Point execution path,
real external tool execution, sandboxing, and a multi-instance worker
process framework are still ahead.

**The Workflow Engine's own step-execution machinery is now constructed
in `bootstrap.py` too, with one minimal real trigger reaching it.**
`_lifespan` now builds the database engine once itself (its own
`try`/`except`, separate from the LLM-specific one inside
`_build_prompted_agent_registry`, since a real database with only a
missing/bad LLM secret should still leave a real engine for the
Workflow Engine to use — previously these two independent failure modes
were collapsed into one). Given that engine and whatever
`AgentRegistry` `_build_prompted_agent_registry` returned (real or
degraded-empty), `_build_workflow_trigger()` composes a real
`WorkflowInstanceService` (`SqlWorkflowInstanceRepository`,
`SqlWorkflowDefinitionCatalog`, `DispatchingStepExecutor` over
`AgentStepExecutor`/`ToolStepExecutor`/`NoOpStepExecutor`) and a real
`WorkflowAdvanceRunner`, and closes over one small, honestly-named demo
`WorkflowDefinition` (`platform.prompted_agent_smoke_test`, one agent
step naming `platform/prompted-agent`) — constructed directly in Python,
not loaded via `WorkflowDefinitionLoader`, since that loader's own
design is for loading a real pack's own definition file, and there is
no real Capability Pack directory for one to live in yet (every test in
this codebase already constructs `WorkflowDefinition` objects directly
for the identical reason). The returned `trigger(inputs, principal_id)`
closure is attached as `app.state.trigger_prompted_agent_workflow`: it
calls `WorkflowInstanceService.create_instance()` →`.start()` →
`WorkflowAdvanceRunner.run_to_completion()` — the exact,
already-built-and-tested "create/start/advance one instance" mechanism
from two steps ago, reused unchanged, with no new worker scheduler or
multi-instance queue. **Deliberately not a real HTTP route**: re-reading
`docs/07_api/api_architecture.md` in full (this step's own mandated
3–4 step alignment checkpoint) found that its documented
`POST /api/v1/workflows` contract requires OIDC bearer authentication
and `workflow:start` permission-based authorization via a Security
Manager that does not exist yet in this codebase — building a real,
unauthenticated route would violate that already-approved contract, not
just leave it incomplete. This step's own approved framing anticipated
exactly this fork ("prefer a minimal API route ... otherwise a minimal
explicit service entrypoint that bootstrap owns"), so the entrypoint
form was used instead: a real, callable, composition-root-owned function,
reachable by a test or by a future Security-Manager-fronted route, but
never by an unauthenticated HTTP request. Two new deterministic
integration tests (`tests/integration/test_bootstrap_workflow_trigger.py`,
real Postgres via `testcontainers`, a hand-built Echo-backed
`PromptedAgent` substituted for the real Anthropic one) prove the
machinery genuinely dispatches to whatever agent is registered — one
happy-path run reaching `COMPLETED` with the Echo agent's real output
persisted in `workflow_steps`, one proving `AgentNotRegisteredError`
surfaces as a structured `FAILED` `WorkflowRunResult` rather than a
crash when the demo agent isn't registered. A new opt-in live test
(`tests/integration/test_bootstrap_workflow_trigger_live.py`) goes
through the real composition root itself — `build_app()` +
`_lifespan` — seeds a real `catalog.prompts` row, and drives the demo
workflow to a genuine Anthropic-backed completion.

**The Security Manager gap that step's own alignment checkpoint found
is now closed to exactly the width one route needs.** A new
`ai_os_kernel.security_manager` package adds `Principal`/`PrincipalType`/
`SecurityContext` (identity and computed-permissions shapes, reduced
from ADR-0023's full principal ∩ workflow ∩ agent ∩ tool
monotonic-narrowing chain to just the principal's own role-derived
permissions — that fuller chain needs manifest-declared permissions on
workflows/agents/tools, Capability Manager territory not yet built),
`permissions_for_roles()` (a static role → permission table matching
authentication_authorization.md §4.2, reduced to the two permissions
this step enforces — `workflow:read`, `workflow:start` — rather than
the SDK's full closed vocabulary, since no route yet needs the rest),
and `JWTBearerTokenVerifier` — bearer-token authentication via a
pre-shared HS256 signing key resolved through the existing
`SecretProvider` seam (`secret://env/security/jwt-signing-key`, the
same pattern as the Anthropic API key), **not full OIDC**: no JWKS
fetch, no issuer/audience validation against a live identity provider.
This is the "clean minimal auth boundary that does not violate the
architecture and can later be replaced by full OIDC without rewriting
callers" the approved framing asked for — every caller depends on the
`TokenVerifier` `Protocol`, not on `JWTBearerTokenVerifier` directly, so
a real OIDC-backed implementation later replaces one class, not any
call site. `ai_os_kernel.security_manager.dependencies.require_permission`
composes authentication and one permission check into a single FastAPI
dependency, failing closed throughout: no bearer token → `401`;
invalid/expired/malformed token → `401`; valid token lacking the
required permission → `403`; **no configured signing secret at all →
`503`**, not default-allow — an unconfigured Security Manager must deny
every protected request, the same "fail closed" rule
authentication_authorization.md §3.3/§4.3 states explicitly. `bootstrap.py`
builds this token verifier in `_lifespan` independently of the database
engine (authentication needs neither persistence nor the LLM secret),
attaching it (or `None`, on a missing secret) as `app.state.token_verifier`.
**`POST /api/v1/workflows`** (`ai_os_kernel.routes.workflows`) is the
first real HTTP route in this codebase: it requires `workflow:start`,
then calls the existing `app.state.trigger_prompted_agent_workflow`
unchanged. Deliberately narrower than api_architecture.md §6.1's full
documented contract — no `definition_id`/`definition_version`/
`experiment_id`, no `Idempotency-Key`, and `200` with the real
structured outcome instead of `202` fire-and-forget, since the
underlying trigger still runs the one demo workflow synchronously to
completion (no worker loop exists yet, an unchanged, already-documented
gap) — returning `202` for work already finished by the time the
handler returns would misrepresent what actually happens. Two new test
layers prove this without needing a live Anthropic credential:
`tests/unit/kernel/security_manager/` and
`tests/unit/kernel/routes/test_workflows.py` (24 tests, no database, no
network — permission math, JWT verification edge cases, and the full
401/403/503 decision tree against a real `bootstrap.build_app()` with
no real database configured) and
`tests/integration/test_workflows_route.py` (2 tests, real Postgres via
`testcontainers`, no Anthropic credential — an authorized request
genuinely reaches the real Workflow Engine and gets back a real,
structured `failed` outcome since the demo agent isn't registered
without a real LLM secret, proving the security boundary does not
block or distort a legitimate request while staying deterministic; an
unauthorized request is rejected before the engine is ever touched). A
new opt-in live test, `tests/integration/test_workflows_route_live.py`,
drives the demo workflow to a genuine Anthropic-backed `completed`
result through the real HTTP route with a real bearer token — the full
stack, end to end, for the first time.

**A client can now read back what it created, not just receive the
synchronous response of creating it.** `POST /api/v1/workflows`
returns its own result once, at trigger time; nothing let a caller
re-fetch a workflow instance's current state, per-step history, or
event log afterward. Three new routes close that specific gap — `GET
/api/v1/workflows/{id}`, `.../steps`, `.../events` — reusing the
already-built `WorkflowInstanceRepository.get_instance()`/
`list_steps()`/`list_events()` read accessors unchanged, gated on the
`workflow:read` permission the previous step's role table already
granted `viewer` (and every other role). No new Security Manager
concept was needed. All three return the real domain models
(`WorkflowInstance`, `WorkflowStepRecord`, `WorkflowEventRecord`)
directly rather than a translated DTO, and all three return `404` for
a `workflow_id` naming no real instance — a plain repository read
finding nothing is not the same condition as the Workflow Engine being
unavailable (`503`, when no repository was constructed at all, e.g. no
real database configured). The events route is deliberately not
cursor-paginated, unlike api_architecture.md §9's documented shape for
collections generally — building one endpoint's worth of cursor
pagination ahead of every other collection endpoint needing the
identical mechanism is a distinct, later step.

**That distinct, later step is now done — for the one collection that
mattered most.** `GET /api/v1/workflows` lists every workflow instance,
newest first, cursor-paginated exactly as api_architecture.md §9
documents (`{"items": [...], "next_cursor": "…" | null}`), never
offset-paginated — offsets would skip or duplicate a row inserted
between two page requests against a table that grows continuously,
precisely the failure mode §9 rules offsets out for. The cursor is a
keyset position (`created_at` + `workflow_id`, the latter a ULID and
therefore itself time-sortable — a natural tiebreaker for rows sharing
one timestamp), compared with a genuine row-wise `<` comparison against
the existing `ix_workflow_instances_created_at_desc` index, not two
independent column filters that could double-count a row. Encoding that
keyset into an opaque, base64-encoded cursor string is the HTTP layer's
job (`ai_os_kernel.routes.workflows`); comparing two keysets is the
repository's (`WorkflowInstanceRepository.list_instances()`, a new
method following the same "plain, unguarded read" shape `get_instance`/
`list_steps`/`list_events` already established). Gated on the same
`workflow:read` permission the instance-detail routes already use — no
new Security Manager concept, no new role, no new permission. `limit`
is bounded (`1`–`100`, default `50`, api_architecture.md §9's own
example) by FastAPI's own `Query` validation, returning `422` outside
that range; a malformed cursor returns `400` before any query runs.
Deliberately no `status`/`definition_id`/`created_after` filters (§5's
own documented examples) and no query DSL — this step is a *correct
list endpoint*, not search; filtering is a distinct, later step layered
on the same cursor mechanism.

**Pack activation stops being a fiction only tests could produce.**
`SqlAgentRegistry`/`SqlToolRegistry` have gated agent/tool resolution on
`catalog.packs.state` being `activated` since an earlier step, but
nothing in this codebase could ever get a pack into that state except a
test hand-writing a row with raw SQL — the Capability Manager itself
was still an empty, docstring-only placeholder. A new
`ai_os_kernel.capability_manager` module changes that: `register()`
installs a real pack row, `activate()`/`deactivate()` transition it,
and every transition is recorded in `catalog.pack_state_transitions`
— capability_manager.md §4's "every transition is recorded ... with
actor, reason, and timestamp" now genuinely happens, for the first
time, rather than being an unenforced aspiration. This is deliberately
the smallest useful slice of the full canonical eight-state lifecycle
(`discovered → validated → installed → configured → activated →
{deactivated, failed} → uninstalled`): three operations
(register/install, activate, deactivate), no `configured`/`failed`/
`uninstalled` transitions, no version-upgrade path, no health
monitoring, no permissions matrix, no sandboxing, no remote/marketplace
installation, and no new HTTP surface (no `/api/v1/packs/...` route —
out of scope, this step is a pure Python write path). A pack activated
through the new writer gates `SqlAgentRegistry` exactly as a hand-seeded
row already did — proven directly, not assumed, by a new integration
test that registers and activates a pack through the new writer, then
resolves a real agent through the unchanged `SqlAgentRegistry`, then
deactivates it and confirms resolution is refused again.

**That writer is now reachable from the real composition root, not
only from a test constructing it directly.** `kernel/bootstrap.py`'s
`_lifespan` now attaches `app.state.pack_lifecycle_repository` — a
real `SqlPackLifecycleRepository` over the same engine
`workflow_instance_repository` already uses — whenever a real database
engine is available, degrading to simply absent (never a crash) when
one is not, the identical pattern every other Stage B/C `app.state`
attribute in this file already follows. No new HTTP route reads it
(deliberately out of scope, per this step's own framing) — a caller
today is a test with the real `app.state` in hand; a future route or
CLI command is the natural next caller. A new integration test proves
the real composition root itself, not a hand-assembled equivalent,
genuinely registers, activates, and deactivates a pack against a real
(`testcontainers`) Postgres — the same "prove it through `build_app()`
itself" discipline already applied to `app.state.agent_registry` and
`app.state.trigger_prompted_agent_workflow` before it.

**That caller now exists: an authenticated HTTP surface.** Two new
permissions — `pack:read`/`pack:manage` (api_architecture.md §5: "Read
/ install, activate, deactivate packs") — are granted only to
`maintainer`/`admin`, exactly matching authentication_authorization.md
§4.2's role table, which mentions packs at all only starting from
`maintainer` ("`operator` + install/activate/deactivate packs").
`ai_os_kernel.routes.packs` adds four routes reusing
`app.state.pack_lifecycle_repository` unchanged: `POST /api/v1/packs`
(register/install — not in api_architecture.md §6.5's documented list
at all, a reasoned addition following the same resource-creation
convention `POST /api/v1/workflows`/`POST /api/v1/experiments` already
use, since the docs never specified a route shape for registering a
pack in the first place), `.../activate`, `.../deactivate` (both
returning `200` with the real, complete `PackRecord` rather than §6.5's
documented `202` — the identical "don't claim accepted-for-later when
the work already finished synchronously" reasoning already applied to
`POST /api/v1/workflows`), and `GET /api/v1/packs/{id}` (`200`,
matching §6.5 exactly). `PackAlreadyRegisteredError`/
`InvalidPackTransitionError` map to `409` (api_architecture.md §8's own
"state conflict" status, the same one a conflicting approval decision
already uses); `PackNotFoundError` maps to `404`.

**A full Architecture Alignment Checkpoint was performed before this
step's implementation began** — required by this step's own approved
framing, re-reading the Constitution, System Architecture, Kernel
Architecture, LLM Gateway Architecture (the Router's actual home — see
below), Capability Manager Architecture, Security Architecture, and
Coding Standards in full. One real finding: **"Router Architecture" is
not a standalone document.** The Router is documented as one of the
LLM Gateway's thirteen internal subsystems (llm_gateway.md §3: "alias →
provider/model, fallback chain"), not a Kernel-level component
alongside Workflow Engine/Capability Manager/Security Manager —
kernel_architecture.md's own component list never mentions a Router at
all. The correct home for this step's implementation is therefore
`ai_os_kernel.llm_gateway.router`, not a new top-level `ai_os_kernel.router`
package; building the latter would have been exactly the kind of
architectural drift this checkpoint exists to catch. No other drift,
ADR violation, or documentation inconsistency was found — see §5 for
the full checkpoint result.

**The Router itself promotes an already-flagged temporary seam, rather
than inventing a new one.** `AnthropicAdapter` used to resolve
`model_alias -> model_id` through a flat dict it owned directly — its
own docstring, `model_config.py`'s own docstring, and `config/llm.yaml`'s
own comment had all *already* named this "the Router (beyond the flat
alias lookup...)" and "deliberately NOT the documented Router config
shape," explicitly deferred, not forgotten. `ai_os_kernel.llm_gateway.router`
closes that deferral to exactly the width this step approves: a
`Router` Protocol (`resolve(model_alias) -> RoutingDecision`) and one
deterministic implementation, `StaticRouter`, built from the identical
`config/llm.yaml` alias mapping unchanged. `RoutingDecision` is
deliberately a single resolved choice, not a chain — llm_gateway.md
§7's documented `aliases.<alias>.chain` list is for the *Retry &
Fallback Manager* to walk on failure, a separate, later subsystem this
step does not build. `AnthropicAdapter` now depends on an injected
`Router` instead of a raw mapping, and refuses clearly (rather than
silently routing around) if a `Router` ever names a provider other than
the one it serves — the one provider that exists today, but a real
guard against tomorrow's second one.

**"Tomorrow's second one" arrived one step later, deliberately without
a second real network-calling adapter.** `StaticRouter`'s own
`default_provider` was itself a narrower shape than the architecture
calls for — one provider applied to every alias, not a per-alias
choice — so this step closed that gap: `StaticRouter` now takes
`routes: Mapping[str, RoutingDecision]` directly, each alias carrying
its own provider, genuinely multi-provider rather than
provider-uniform. Proving that mechanism needed a real second
`LLMGateway` implementation to dispatch to, and one already existed:
`EchoLLMGateway` (llm_gateway.md's own trivial, always-real
implementation) is not a mock built for this test — it is a second,
genuine `LLMGateway`. The new `DispatchingLLMGateway` asks the Router
which provider an alias means and delegates to whichever real gateway
is registered under that name, refusing clearly if none is. No health
scoring, no retry-on-failure, no fallback walking — the one decision
made is entirely the Router's, this class only acts on it, per this
step's own "keep all routing decisions encapsulated inside the Router"
requirement. Wired into the real composition root
(`build_anthropic_prompted_completion_service` now wraps the Anthropic
adapter in a `DispatchingLLMGateway` with exactly one provider
registered), so today's behaviour is unchanged — the extension point a
future second real provider adapter would register into now exists in
one place.

**That extension point now has a second real occupant.** No official
vendor SDK exists for a self-hosted model server the way `anthropic`
exists for Anthropic, so `LocalAdapter` speaks the OpenAI-compatible
`/v1/chat/completions` HTTP contract self-hosted servers (Ollama, vLLM,
llama.cpp's server, LM Studio, ...) already converge on — a real,
already-standard wire protocol, using `httpx` directly (now a direct
dependency, not only a transitive one via `anthropic`) rather than
inventing or importing a vendor SDK that does not exist. Reaching this
adapter needed one small, additive extension to `config/llm.yaml`:
a `providers:` section (alias -> provider name, for any alias that is
not the Anthropic default) and a `local_provider.base_url` section —
both optional, so every alias and every test that predates this step
is completely unaffected. `kernel/bootstrap.py` builds a real
`LocalAdapter` from `local_provider.base_url` and hands it to
`build_anthropic_prompted_completion_service` (extended with one new,
defaulted-`None` `additional_gateways` parameter) only when that
section is present — absent by default, so this changes no observable
behaviour for any deployment that configures nothing new. A dedicated
test (`test_multi_provider_routing.py`) proves one real `StaticRouter`
and one real `DispatchingLLMGateway` genuinely reaching **two**
different real, network-calling provider adapters for two different
aliases, each adapter talking to its own real local HTTP endpoint —
the concrete demonstration this step's own approved framing required.

**Two real providers made a real chain worth building.** With only
Anthropic registered, a fallback candidate would always have named the
same provider that just failed for the same reason (or an
unregistered one) — genuinely low-value. `RoutingDecision` gained one
new, optional, recursive field: `fallback: RoutingDecision | None`, so
a chain of any length is just a linked list of the same object that
already existed, and `build_routing_chain()` builds one from an
ordered `(provider, model_id)` sequence without a caller hand-nesting
the construction. `DispatchingLLMGateway` — already the seam that acts
on the Router's decision — now also walks `fallback` on any
`LLMProviderError`, chain traversal only: no backoff (no delay between
attempts), no circuit breaker (no per-provider failure memory across
calls), matching this step's own exclusions and llm_gateway.md §3's
own three-part description of the subsystem ("backoff, circuit
breaker, chain traversal") by building exactly the third part. An alias
with no configured chain gets exactly its previous, single-candidate
behaviour — a failure raises the identical, unwrapped error, proven by
a dedicated test asserting the exact same exception object propagates.
A response that only succeeded on a fallback candidate has its
`usage.fallback_used` honestly set to `True` (llm_gateway.md §5's own
field, previously always `False` for lack of a real fallback to set
it); exhausting every candidate raises one clear, aggregate error
naming every provider tried. A model's real refusal
(`LLMRefusalError`) deliberately does not trigger a fallback — a
refusal is a valid response, not a failure to route around.

**A chain that always tries every candidate, forever, was the next
honest gap — the Circuit Breaker closes it.** Before this step, a
provider having a genuinely bad day (repeated timeouts, an outage) was
retried on every single request, wasting a real network round trip on
a call already very likely to fail, every time. `CircuitBreaker` (a
Protocol, so a persisted/shared implementation can substitute later —
ADR-0004) and its one real implementation, `InMemoryCircuitBreaker`,
give `DispatchingLLMGateway` a cheap way to skip a candidate it already
knows is unhealthy: closed and healthy by default, opens after a
configured run of consecutive failures, and half-opens on a timer to
test recovery — llm_gateway.md §10's own three-state description,
exactly. The timer transition is lazy (checked, not scheduled) — no
new background thread, consistent with this codebase's existing "no
worker scheduler" stance elsewhere. Wiring it into `DispatchingLLMGateway`
needed no change to the chain-walking logic itself: a circuit-open skip
raises the identical `LLMProviderError` shape a real failure already
does, so the existing fallback/aggregate-error code path handles both
uniformly, without knowing or caring which happened.

**Chain traversal and the Circuit Breaker both move on immediately
after one failed attempt — backoff is what makes "one attempt" mean
something closer to a real, resilient call.** A transient blip (a
single dropped connection, one momentary 5xx) previously exhausted a
candidate immediately and moved straight to the next one, even though
the identical candidate would very likely have succeeded a moment
later. `BackoffPolicy` (llm_gateway.md §10: "exponential backoff with
jitter, bounded attempts and total time") gives `DispatchingLLMGateway`
a bounded number of same-candidate retries, with a growing, jittered
delay between them, before it gives up on that candidate and moves to
its fallback — provider-level retry only, never a whole-chain retry
loop, and never a second layer stacked on the Workflow Engine's own
step-level retry (§10's own "a single boundary, so retries cannot
multiply"). It cooperates with the Circuit Breaker directly, not just
by coincidence: before sleeping for a retry, the breaker is consulted
again, so a candidate whose circuit the very first failure already
opened is not retried further — no wasted delay on a call already
known to be rejected. A response that only succeeded after a retry has
its `usage.retries` honestly set for the first time; previously always
`0` for lack of a real retry to count.

**Every `LLMProviderError` was treated identically — chain traversal,
the Circuit Breaker, and backoff all retried/counted/fell back on
*anything*, uniformly, with no distinction between "the provider is
briefly overloaded" and "the caller sent a malformed request that will
never succeed."** That undifferentiated treatment was itself a real,
documented gap this project's own docs called for closing:
llm_gateway.md §10 (and `error_handling_retry.md` §3, "the single
platform error taxonomy") both describe exactly which provider
conditions are `transient`/`permanent`/`infrastructure`/`budget`, and
whether each is worth retrying. New `ai_os_kernel.llm_gateway.
error_taxonomy` closes that gap for the LLM Gateway specifically
(without building the platform-wide `AiOsError` hierarchy those
documents also describe — that would reach into the Workflow Engine,
Quality Gate Engine, and Security Manager, all out of scope for a step
scoped to "keep error classification encapsulated inside the LLM
Gateway"). Both real adapters now classify every failure they raise
using the exact table llm_gateway.md §10 documents, extracting a real
`Retry-After` header when a provider sends one. Backoff now skips
retrying a `retriable=False` failure immediately (an invalid request,
an unsupported capability — retrying would only ever reproduce the
identical failure) while still letting the chain fall back to a
*different* provider, which might not share the same problem. The
Circuit Breaker now only counts a `transient`/`infrastructure` failure
— a caller's own malformed request is not evidence the *provider* is
unhealthy, and counting it would let one bad request wrongly trip a
circuit that would otherwise serve every other, valid request fine.
Every default (`category=transient`, `retriable=True`) reproduces the
exact undifferentiated behaviour every pre-existing call site (and
every pre-existing test) already assumed — nothing changes for a
failure this step does not explicitly reclassify.

**Every real adapter already computed an honest `usage.cost_usd` — nothing yet used it for anything but observability.** llm_gateway.md §3 documents a Policy & Budget Enforcer (§9: step/workflow/experiment cost ceilings, allowed-model policy, per-principal rate limits, context-window fit, all "enforced before the provider call"), and `ErrorCategory.BUDGET` already existed in the new Error Taxonomy with nothing raising it. New `ai_os_kernel.llm_gateway.budget_enforcer` closes the smallest real piece of that gap: a per-`model_alias` cumulative-cost ceiling, since `LLMRequest`'s own deliberately reduced contract carries no workflow correlation for a true per-workflow ceiling to attach to (extending it would be Context Manager/Workflow Engine territory, out of scope for a step scoped to the LLM Gateway). Reusing cost data that already exists needed no new estimation logic — the enforcer only ever accumulates a real, already-computed number and compares it to a ceiling. The one genuinely new behaviour decision: a budget failure never triggers a fallback attempt, unlike every other category — a ceiling is scoped to the alias itself, not to whichever provider would happen to serve it, so trying a different candidate could never be the fix.

**The workflow-correlation gap the previous step named as its own limitation is now closed, using the exact mechanism platform_sdk.md §4.1 documents for it — not a Gateway-invented field.** A direct architectural check, requested before any code, confirmed `TraceContext` (`trace_id; span_id; workflow_id?; step_id?; agent_id?; experiment_id?; run_id?` — platform_sdk.md §4.1's own words: "Field names are normative") is the documented, canonical, multi-consumer shape (also used by `AgentRequest`, `ToolRequest`, `StructuredError`), not an LLM-Gateway-local convenience — so this step implements a deliberately minimal slice of that real contract rather than inventing standalone `workflow_id`/`step_id` fields on `LLMRequest`, exactly the direction the approving message asked to have validated. New `TraceContext` (in `llm_gateway/models.py`, alongside `LLMRequest` — no shared cross-component module yet, since `LLMRequest.metadata` is still its only real consumer, ADR-0004) carries only `workflow_id`/`step_id`; the other five documented fields are absent as fields entirely, not present as always-`None` ones, since an unpopulated field for a capability nothing feeds would itself be the "placeholder architecture" this step must avoid. `LLMRequest` gained `metadata: TraceContext | None = None`, llm_gateway.md §4's own documented field name. `PromptedCompletionService.complete_from_prompt` already accepted `workflow_id`/`step_id` (for the call recorder); it now also builds a `TraceContext` from them and sets it as `metadata` — no new parameter, only new behaviour, so every existing caller passing neither keeps getting `metadata=None`, byte-for-byte unchanged. The previous step's `PerAliasBudgetEnforcer` was already internally scope-agnostic (keyed by a generic `scope: str`); it is renamed to `PerScopeBudgetEnforcer` (a recorded decision, not a silent rename) and instantiated a second, fully independent time — a distinct ceiling, a distinct scope space, keyed by `workflow_id` instead of `model_alias` — giving `DispatchingLLMGateway` a genuine, working per-workflow cost ceiling (llm_gateway.md §9's "Workflow cost ceiling" row) for the first time, without duplicating any accounting logic. A budget failure from either enforcer already shared one category-based "never falls back, never retried" gate in `complete()`, built the previous step — it required no new code to also cover the new ceiling, a small validation of that design. `kernel/bootstrap.py` now wires both: `_ALIAS_BUDGET_CEILING_USD = Decimal("10.00")` (unchanged) and `_WORKFLOW_BUDGET_CEILING_USD = Decimal("25.00")` (new), as two independent `PerScopeBudgetEnforcer` instances. Two things surfaced worth recording rather than silently resolving: platform_sdk.md §4.2's `AgentRequest` shape lists `workflow_id`/`step_id` both as top-level fields *and* nested inside its own `trace: TraceContext` — a real documentation redundancy, not resolved here (out of scope, `AgentRequest` is not touched by this step) — and `trace_id`/`span_id` already have real values available today via this Kernel's own OpenTelemetry span instrumentation (ADR-0017), but reading that span context and threading it into `TraceContext` is a distinct, later Observability-integration step, not this one, so those two fields stay absent rather than becoming a third, cross-cutting concern smuggled into a Policy & Budget step.

**An architecture review of the LLM Gateway's remaining work concluded it had reached a genuine stopping point, and the Context Manager — a Stage-B component listed alongside the LLM Gateway in kernel_architecture.md's own build order, until now entirely unbuilt — is now the more urgent gap.** Every LLM Gateway capability still missing was found to be either blocked on architecture outside the Gateway (allowed-model policy and per-principal rate limiting need Security Context propagation that stops at `WorkflowInstance.principal_id` today; per-step and per-experiment budgets need a Step Contract budget field and a Benchmarking Pack, neither of which exist) or a genuinely new core-contract capability (tool-calling, structured output, streaming) with no real consumer yet to prove it against. Meanwhile, direct inspection of `AgentStepExecutor`/`PromptedAgent` found that the real Workflow Engine path had **no source of prompt template variables at all** — `WorkflowStep` declares none, and nothing populated `inputs["variables"]` outside a test hand-building it — so every prompt rendered through a real workflow instance necessarily rendered with zero variables. New `ai_os_kernel.context_manager` closes the smallest real piece of this: one real resolver (`WorkflowStateResolver`, reading a workflow instance's own declared `inputs`) behind a real `ContextManager`/`DefaultContextManager` abstraction, wired into `AgentStepExecutor` (which now also receives `workflow_id` from `WorkflowInstanceService.advance()`, an additive `StepExecutor.execute()` signature extension) and consumed by `PromptedAgent` — agent_architecture.md's first real "Context Consumer," flattening assembled items into one `context` prompt template variable. Proven not by construction alone but by a real-database integration test: a workflow's own `inputs`, written to a real Postgres row, read back by the real resolver, and found verbatim in the agent's final rendered output. Five of context_manager.md §3's six documented sources (Knowledge Manager, Memory Manager, AI Context Packs, Runtime Configuration, and a distinct "User-provided inputs" path) remain unbuilt or deferred, and no filtering, ranking, or budget enforcement exists yet — this step establishes the abstraction and the request flow, not breadth.

**A follow-up architecture review re-evaluated whether the Context Manager or another subsystem was now the higher priority, from scratch rather than defaulting to the previously-recommended Runtime Configuration Resolver, and found a stronger, more specific signal than either option: implementation_roadmap.md §3's own Stage B deliverable line for this component reads "Context Manager with deterministic assembly, budget enforcement, and trust tagging."** Deterministic assembly and trust tagging were already done; budget enforcement was the one named, in-stage, unmet piece — a smaller, more explicitly-documented target than Runtime Configuration (not named in the roadmap at all) and unblocked, unlike Security Context propagation (explicitly a Stage C deliverable per the same roadmap document, not yet due) or Knowledge/Memory retrieval (blocked on an entirely unbuilt Platform Service, ADR-0013). New: `ContextRequest.token_budget` (optional, rejects `<= 0`) and `DefaultContextManager`'s own `default_token_budget`, admitting items by descending `relevance_score` (a stable sort, ties preserve resolver order — ADR-0022) while returning survivors in their original resolver order, and reporting a now-real `items_excluded_count`. `kernel/bootstrap.py` wires one named ceiling (`_CONTEXT_TOKEN_BUDGET`); no Workflow Engine file changed.

**A third architecture review re-evaluated the entire roadmap rather than defaulting to another Context Manager resolver, and found the LLM Gateway's own "capability matrix" — implementation_roadmap.md §3's Stage B deliverable line for the Gateway — was the smallest, most explicitly-named, currently-unblocked remaining piece anywhere in Stage B.** Quality Gate Engine and Tool Invoker, though nominally Stage B-listed, were traced to ADR-0006/ADR-0016 dependencies that pace their *real* substance to Stage C (no generated code exists yet to gate or sandbox); Retrieval and Platform SDK are Stage B-named but large enough to need their own dedicated scoping; Event Bus has no current real consumer anywhere in the implemented system. New `ai_os_kernel.llm_gateway.capability_negotiator`: `ProviderCapabilities` (llm_gateway.md §6's full matrix), a `CapabilityNegotiator` Protocol, and `StaticCapabilityNegotiator` — resolves an alias to a model id via the real `Router`, then looks up a real, per-model-id matrix sourced from a new `config/llm.yaml` `capabilities:` section (the identical shape `pricing` already established). `DispatchingLLMGateway` gained an optional `capability_negotiator` and a new public, synchronous `capabilities(alias)` method — deliberately not added to the shared `LLMGateway` Protocol yet, the same precedent `stream()`/`embed()`/`count_tokens()` already set. Nothing consumes the matrix: no tool-calling, structured-output emulation, streaming, or context-window-fit check exists to ask it anything yet — this step is the lookup only, not "emulate or fail."

**With every small, explicitly roadmap-named, unblocked Stage B increment exhausted (§6's own prior finding), Retrieval — the single most-cited blocker across three consecutive architecture reviews — was scoped down to its own first real increment: the Knowledge Schema and Database Migration (data_model.md §7), persistence only, no search logic.** A first validation pass stopped before writing any code, having found an apparent conflict (`knowledge.embeddings.embedding`'s documented `vector(N)` type requires the pgvector Postgres extension, seemingly contradicting a "no hidden dependency on pgvector" check) and a documentation-naming discrepancy (the referenced `retrieval.md` does not exist; the real document is `search_vector_search.md`). A required second, deeper review found `infra/docker-compose.yml`, `deployment_architecture.md` §5, and ADR-0013 already establish pgvector as an Accepted, already-provisioned Stage-A decision — resolving the apparent conflict as a sequencing issue the first pass's own validation should have caught by checking the infra layer, not a real one. New `ai_os_kernel.persistence.knowledge_schema` module and `0029_knowledge_schema` migration implement all four documented tables (`documents`, `chunks`, `embeddings`, `memory_items`), enable the `vector` extension, and add the new `pgvector` Python dependency (ADR-0009). `embeddings.embedding` is a deliberately unconstrained `Vector()` (data_model.md §7 names no concrete dimension `N`, and the table's own per-row `dimensions` column confirms varying real sizes are the intended design), with the documented HNSW index deliberately deferred until a real embedding-model dimension is chosen — a decision made and recorded during implementation itself, reasoned as having exactly one non-speculative answer rather than requiring a third stop-and-report cycle. Proven via 14 new real-database integration tests (Docker Desktop unavailable this session — a third consecutive step — so also cross-checked via direct DDL compilation).

**Retrieval's schema-only increment is now followed by its own smallest real writer, per the established "schema, then a minimal writer" pattern this document's own §6 named as the next step.** New `ai_os_kernel.persistence.knowledge_writer` module (`KnowledgeWriter` Protocol, `SqlKnowledgeWriter`) accepts already-chunked input (a document's `source_uri`/`content_hash`/`media_type`/`trust`/`project_id` plus an ordered sequence of already-produced chunks) and persists one `knowledge.documents` row and its `knowledge.chunks` rows in a single transaction — no chunking, hashing, embedding, search, or ingestion logic of its own, exactly this step's own approved fence. Deliberately placed beside `knowledge_schema.py` in `persistence/`, not inside the still-untouched `knowledge_manager` stub package — reasoned as the identical "own writer module, no owning domain component built yet" shape already established for `workflow_engine.definition_catalog` and `llm_gateway.call_recorder`, since integrating into `knowledge_manager` itself was explicitly out of this step's own scope. `document_id`/`chunk_id` are freshly generated on every call (new `knowledge_ids.py`, `doc_`/`chunk_` prefixes) — not an idempotent upsert, since a document being ingested has no natural stable business key the way a workflow definition's own declared `id` does. `ordinal` is derived from the caller's own list order via `enumerate()`, not a separate caller-supplied field. An empty chunk sequence, or any blank required field, is rejected before a transaction opens; a real check-constraint violation (e.g. an invalid `trust` value) is wrapped in a new `KnowledgeWriteError`, never a bare stack trace. 10 new real-database integration tests (`tests/integration/persistence/test_knowledge_writer.py`), including a proof that `content_tsv` still genuinely generates through this write path (not just through a raw `INSERT`, extending `test_migrations.py`'s own proof of the column itself). Docker Desktop unavailable again this session (a fourth consecutive step) — cross-checked instead via direct compilation of the writer's own `INSERT` statements against the PostgreSQL dialect (confirming the generated `content_tsv` column is correctly excluded from the compiled column list, exactly as it would be at runtime) and direct execution of the writer's pure-Python validation/ordinal logic against a stub engine. No consumer exists yet (no Indexing Pipeline, no Knowledge Manager, no API route) — `kernel/bootstrap.py` is deliberately untouched this step, since wiring a writer nothing calls would be dead composition-root code, not real behaviour change.

**With a real writer producing real `knowledge.chunks` rows, Retrieval's third increment builds the smallest real reader over them: a Keyword Search component, search_vector_search.md's own "Postgres FTS: tsvector + GIN" line, made real for the first time.** New `ai_os_kernel.persistence.knowledge_keyword_search` module (`KeywordSearcher` Protocol, `SqlKeywordSearcher`) ranks matching chunks for a plain-text query via `plainto_tsquery`/`ts_rank` against the already-existing `content_tsv` generated column and its GIN index — no new index, no schema change. `plainto_tsquery` (not `websearch_to_tsquery`'s richer phrase/exclusion syntax, nor `to_tsquery`'s raw boolean operators) was chosen as the simplest, safest match for "accepts a plain-text query string." The query is parsed with the identical `'english'` configuration `content_tsv` was generated with — not a second independent choice, since a mismatched configuration would silently stop matching rows it should. Results are ordered by `ts_rank` descending with `chunk_id` (a sortable ULID) as a deterministic tiebreak (ADR-0022). A blank query or non-positive `limit` is rejected before any query runs; `limit` defaults to a named `_DEFAULT_LIMIT = 10` constant, overridable per call. Deliberately placed beside `knowledge_writer.py`/`knowledge_schema.py` in `persistence/`, not inside `knowledge_manager` or a new "retrieval" package — the identical "bare persistence-layer reader, no owning domain component built yet" reasoning already applied to the writer, mirroring `SqlPromptCatalog`'s own thin-reader shape. 10 new real-database integration tests (`tests/integration/persistence/test_knowledge_keyword_search.py`) seed real rows through the previous step's own `SqlKnowledgeWriter` (not hand-written SQL) — proving the two increments compose — covering: matching/ranking/exclusion, an explicit and the documented default `limit`, English stemming agreement between generation and query parsing, deterministic tie ordering across repeated identical queries, and rejection of blank/invalid input. No vector/hybrid search, no RRF, no `knowledge.embeddings` involvement, no metadata/`trust`/`project_id` filtering, no HTTP route, no Context Manager resolver, no Knowledge Manager integration — exactly this step's own approved fence. Docker Desktop unavailable again this session (a fifth consecutive step) — cross-checked instead by compiling the reader's exact `SELECT`/`WHERE`/`ORDER BY` statement against the PostgreSQL dialect, confirming it renders valid `ts_rank(...)`/`plainto_tsquery(...)`/`@@` SQL with the expected bind parameters.

**Priority reprioritized by the product owner: deepening Retrieval/LLM Gateway/Security is paused; the new priority is the shortest real path to a working multi-agent software-engineering pipeline (Architecture Agent → Build Agents → Test Agents → Documentation Agent).** Nothing built under the previous priority is removed — every Retrieval/LLM Gateway/Context Manager/Capability Manager increment above remains real and in place; this changes only what gets built next. The first increment under the new priority is a minimal Tier 1 Sandbox Executor (ADR-0016), since Build/Test Agents cannot run generated code at all without one, and it is explicitly named as a Stage-C-paced item this project has deferred since the very first review that surveyed it. **A genuine tension with the governing architecture was found and resolved, not silently avoided.** ADR-0016's own "Alternatives Considered" section rejects "Direct subprocess execution with a restricted user" outright — "shares the kernel, filesystem namespace, and network namespace with the platform, and offers no meaningful containment for hostile code" — yet this step's own approved framing explicitly asks for a real `LocalSubprocessSandbox` backend "per ADR-0016's Tier 1 guarantees." Resolution: implement the subprocess backend for real, but make it **honestly declare, not silently claim, which of ADR-0016's controls it actually enforces** — new `SandboxGuarantees` (`enforces_timeout`/`enforces_output_cap`/`enforces_secret_exclusion`, all `True` for this backend; `enforces_network_isolation`/`enforces_filesystem_containment`, both `False`, truthfully), mirroring the LLM Gateway's own `ProviderCapabilities` "declare a real, checkable matrix" precedent. New `ai_os_kernel.sandbox` package: `SandboxExecutor` Protocol (`execute(*, command, working_directory, timeout_seconds, max_output_bytes, env=None) -> SandboxResult`) and `LocalSubprocessSandbox`, its first real backend — genuinely enforcing secret exclusion (the child never inherits `os.environ`; a small explicit allowlist plus caller-opted-in `env` only), a real wall-clock timeout, and a real per-stream output cap that proactively kills the process the instant either stream exceeds it (rather than waiting out the full timeout while the child blocks on an undrained pipe) — and never using `shell=True` (security_architecture.md §15). No Docker/OCI backend, no Tool, no Tool Invoker wiring, no Capability Pack — exactly this step's own approved fence. A real, Windows-`ProactorEventLoop`-specific bug was found and fixed during implementation (not left in): cancelling a task blocked on a subprocess pipe read does not reliably unblock it on Windows — the fix kills the process *before* cancelling, which closes the pipes and lets everything resolve promptly, rather than relying on cancellation alone. Proven by 18 new **unit** tests (`tests/unit/kernel/sandbox/test_executor.py`) — genuinely executed this session (no Docker/Postgres dependency at all, the first fully-verified-live test suite in five consecutive steps) — including a real secrets-isolation proof (a secret set in the *test process's own* environment is confirmed absent from the sandboxed child) and a real shell-injection-immunity proof (a payload containing `$(...)`/`` `...` ``/`&&` is printed back verbatim, never evaluated).

**With a real Sandbox Executor now in place, the second increment under the new priority builds the smallest real thing that uses it: a genuine `tier1_sandboxed` Tool, and the `ToolStepExecutor` change needed to actually dispatch to it.** Direct inspection confirmed the exact named blocker this closes: `ToolStepExecutor` previously refused *every* `tier1_sandboxed` tool outright, unconditionally — its own prior docstring said so plainly ("no sandbox exists yet"). New `ai_os_kernel.workflow_engine.sandboxed_tool.SandboxedCommandTool`: a real `Tool` (`trust_tier` fixed to `TIER1_SANDBOXED`, not constructor-settable like `EchoTool`'s, since this Tool's entire behaviour is the thing that tier describes) whose `execute()` delegates to an injected `SandboxExecutor` and maps its `SandboxResult` onto a new, real `output_schema` (`exitCode`/`stdout`/`stderr`/`timedOut`/`truncated`/`durationSeconds`). Its command/working-directory/timeout/output-cap/env are all supplied at **construction**, not per invocation — the already-established "no per-step input-mapping mechanism exists yet" deferral stays on record; `execute(inputs)` still ignores whatever it is called with, exactly like `EchoTool`. **The guard was narrowed, not weakened — verified by construction, not merely by intent.** New `SandboxBackedTool`, a `@runtime_checkable` structural marker Protocol (`ai_os_kernel.workflow_engine.tool`, exposing a `sandbox: SandboxExecutor` attribute) that `SandboxedCommandTool` satisfies by construction. `ToolStepExecutor.execute()` now allows a `tier1_sandboxed` tool through when, and only when, `isinstance(tool, SandboxBackedTool) and tool.sandbox is not None` — a tool merely *claiming* the tier without a real sandbox behind it (the pre-existing `_UntrustedTool` test case, and a new one with a structurally-present-but-`None` `sandbox` attribute) is still refused with the identical `ToolSandboxRequiredError`, unchanged. No relaxation for `tier2_trusted` tools, no change to their own always-allowed path. **No architectural upgrade claimed.** This step does not make the underlying sandbox any more ADR-0016-compliant — `LocalSubprocessSandbox`'s own `guarantees` (previous step) still honestly report no network isolation and no filesystem containment; `SandboxedCommandTool`'s own docstring restates this explicitly, so dispatching through it is not mistaken for a certification the sandbox itself does not make.
