**Assessment:** the persistence, definition-loading, instance-creation, state-transition, step-progression-to-completion, minimal-agent-invocation, minimal-tool-invocation, lease-acquisition-and-renewal, single-shot-claim-and-advance, single-instance-run-to-completion, background-lease-reaping, materialised-per-step-state, minimal-secrets-resolution, real-telemetry-instrumentation (spans and metrics), governance-schema (`audit_log`, `config_changes`, complete), platform-schema (`event_outbox`, `idempotency_keys`; `schema_metadata` deferred, see §4), trace-schema (`artifacts`, `links`, complete), catalog (`workflow_definitions`, `prompts`, `tools`, `agents`, `packs`, and `pack_state_transitions` built — **6 of 6, complete**; the `workflow_instances` → `workflow_definitions` FK is now enforced via a minimal writer, see §3; `prompts` now also has the documented composite `(prompt_id, version)` primary key, closing the gap discovered while building `SqlPromptCatalog` last step, see §3; the four tables' `pack_id` FKs to `packs.pack_id` remain un-retrofitted, a distinct later step, see §4), and evaluation-schema (`run_manifests`, `experiment_runs`, `experiments`, `gate_results`, `metrics`, and now `llm_calls` built — **6 of 6, complete**; `llm_calls.prompt_id`/`prompt_version` now carry a composite FK against `catalog.prompts`' new composite key, `experiments.definition_id`/`definition_version` now carry a composite FK against `catalog.workflow_definitions`, and `metrics.run_id` now carries a FK against `experiment_runs.run_id` — every foreign key data_model.md §6 documents for this schema now exists, see §3) blockers are all closed. `WorkflowAdvanceRunner.run_once()` remains the one production-usable acquire→advance→release unit, `run_to_completion()` drives one instance all the way to `completed` (or a bounded stop, or a reported-not-raised failure) by calling `run_once()` in a loop, `WorkflowLeaseReaper.reap_once()` reclaims expired leases proactively rather than only as a side effect of another worker's `acquire()` call, every executed step leaves a `workflow_steps` row alongside its events rather than living only in the append-only log, and that per-step history is now readable back through `WorkflowInstanceRepository.list_steps()` — together the complete mechanism the roadmap's Stage B exit criterion ("killing the worker mid-step loses no committed state and the workflow resumes on another worker," `implementation_roadmap.md` §3) depends on for a single instance. Every table data_model.md §4 documents for the `workflow` schema now exists, including `approvals` (schema only — no writer yet). Nothing yet calls `run_once`/`reap_once` across *multiple* instances outside of tests, and nothing yet decides how often the reaper should run. The next structural gaps are: (a) letting different steps invoke different agents/tools — `AgentRegistry`/`ToolRegistry` now resolve a step's declared `agentId`/`toolId` to a real registered instance, `AgentStepExecutor`/`ToolStepExecutor` genuinely dispatch by id, and a second, `catalog.agents`/`catalog.tools`-backed implementation of each now confirms a declared id is a real catalog row, confirms its declared pack is a real, `activated` `catalog.packs` row (the new `PackState`/`PackNotActivatedError`, see §3), and genuinely loads and constructs its declared `entrypoint` via `EntrypointLoader` — a real catalog row can now resolve to a real, different implementation, not always `EchoAgent`/`EchoTool`, and only while its pack is active — and a pack can now genuinely become `activated` through a real writer (`ai_os_kernel.capability_manager.SqlPackLifecycleRepository`, see §3) — now constructed in `kernel/bootstrap.py` itself, reachable via `app.state.pack_lifecycle_repository`, and now fronted by four authenticated HTTP routes (`POST /api/v1/packs`, `.../activate`, `.../deactivate`, `GET /api/v1/packs/{id}`, gated on the new `pack:manage`/`pack:read` permissions) — rather than only a test hand-writing a `catalog.packs` row. There is still no pack discovery, no upgrade path, no health monitoring, no permissions enforcement, and no sandboxing. `AgentStepExecutor` now also forwards a step's declared `promptId`/`promptVersion`/`modelAlias` into the resolved agent's `inputs`, and the new `PromptedAgent` is a real agent that uses them — but only when the composition root constructs one directly through `InMemoryAgentRegistry`, since `PromptedAgent`'s real constructor dependencies (a database engine, a resolved secret, model config) are incompatible with `SqlAgentRegistry`'s zero-argument entrypoint loading by the same already-established boundary that excludes real Capability Manager configuration from entrypoints generally. The rest of the Capability Manager (pack discovery, upgrades, permissions, sandboxing, health monitoring, and a `GET /api/v1/packs` list route) remains Stage C, a distinct, later, larger step; (b) real external tool execution and sandboxing — `Tool.execute()` still always receives `{}`, since tool steps have no analogous invocation fields in the Step Contract; (c) the LLM Gateway's remaining internal subsystems (provider health beyond the breaker's own binary signal, experiment pinning, allowed-model policy, per-principal rate limiting, context-window-fit pre-checks — now closer, since a real Capability Negotiator matrix and lookup exist, see §3, but still needing `count_tokens()` — per-step/per-experiment budgets, the four capability degradation rules themselves (the matrix exists, nothing consumes it), tool calling, structured output, caching, streaming, embeddings, `agent_id`/`experiment_id`/`run_id`/`trace_id`/`span_id` on `TraceContext`, the platform-wide `AiOsError` hierarchy) — the `Protocol` seam, a trivial implementation, a minimal Observability writer, **two** real provider adapters (`AnthropicAdapter` and `LocalAdapter`, see §3 — the second speaks the OpenAI-compatible chat-completions protocol self-hosted servers converge on, no vendor SDK needed; both now classify every failure via the `error_taxonomy` module), a real, provable, one-call composition (`build_anthropic_prompted_completion_service()`, accepting `additional_gateways`, `circuit_breaker`, `backoff_policy`, `budget_enforcer`, and `workflow_budget_enforcer`), a real caller of that composition from inside the Workflow Engine (`PromptedAgent`), a genuinely multi-provider Router (`StaticRouter`, per-alias `RoutingDecision`, carrying a real, optional `fallback` chain), request metadata (`TraceContext`, a deliberately minimal slice of platform_sdk.md §4.1's canonical shape carrying `workflow_id`/`step_id`, set as `LLMRequest.metadata`, see §3), and a real dispatcher (`DispatchingLLMGateway` — asks the Router which provider an alias means, first checks an optional alias-scoped `BudgetEnforcer`, then an optional workflow-scoped one populated only when the request's `TraceContext` names a `workflow_id` (two independent per-scope cumulative-cost ceilings, each reusing each adapter's own honest `usage.cost_usd`, neither ever falling back on a budget failure since neither ceiling is provider-scoped), then skips a candidate whose circuit an optional `CircuitBreaker` reports `OPEN` straight to its fallback, otherwise delegates to whichever real gateway is registered under that name — retrying that same candidate per an optional `BackoffPolicy` only when the Error Taxonomy classifies the failure `retriable`, honouring a real `retry_after_seconds` hint, cooperating with the breaker so a retry is skipped once the circuit opens, before giving up — and reports the outcome back to the breaker (only for a `transient`/`infrastructure` classification, not `permanent`), walking the resolved decision's `fallback` chain on any other still-failing `LLMProviderError` — chain traversal, circuit breaking, backoff, and now two real budget gates, all Error-Taxonomy-aware, see §3) plus `CircuitBreaker`/`InMemoryCircuitBreaker`, `BackoffPolicy`, `error_taxonomy` (`ErrorCategory`, `classify_http_status()`, `parse_retry_after_seconds()`, matching llm_gateway.md §10 exactly), and `BudgetEnforcer`/`PerScopeBudgetEnforcer` (renamed from `PerAliasBudgetEnforcer` once genuinely reused for a second, workflow-scoped ceiling, see §3) constructed in `kernel/bootstrap.py` itself, registering `LocalAdapter` alongside `AnthropicAdapter` whenever `config/llm.yaml` configures a `local_provider` (none is, by default, in the checked-in file — so only `anthropic` is registered in the real running Kernel today, degrading to an empty registry rather than crashing when unconfigured; the one real, live-by-default fallback, `coding-strong`, is same-provider, not cross-provider, for the identical reason), the Workflow Engine's own step-execution machinery constructed alongside it with one real, callable trigger (`app.state.trigger_prompted_agent_workflow`), and a minimal Security Manager slice (bearer-token auth + `workflow:start`/`workflow:read`/`pack:read`/`pack:manage` permission checks, see §3) fronting nine real HTTP routes — five workflow routes (`POST`/`GET /api/v1/workflows`, `GET /api/v1/workflows/{id}`/`.../steps`/`.../events`) and four pack routes (`POST /api/v1/packs`, `.../activate`, `.../deactivate`, `GET /api/v1/packs/{id}`), all reusing existing Workflow Engine/Capability Manager components unchanged — exist so far, but every route is deliberately narrower than its documented contract (no `definition_id` selection on the workflow write, no `Idempotency-Key`, no filtering on the workflow list, no pagination on `/steps`/`.../events`, no `GET /api/v1/packs` list, `POST /api/v1/packs` itself undocumented, pack activate/deactivate `200` not `202`), the Security Manager itself is minimal (no full OIDC, no monotonic-narrowing chain, see §3/§4), and every other endpoint §6 documents still does not exist — see "Almost no API surface," §4; (d) the Prompt Engine's remaining internal subsystems (a real Prompt Registry beyond a raw `(prompt_id, version)` lookup, version resolution, role/alias-based Prompt Resolver, prompt composition/inheritance, cache-boundary support, and an Observability writer) — the `Protocol` seam now has two implementations (`InMemoryPromptEngine`, `SqlPromptCatalog`, see §3), `catalog.prompts` now has the composite primary key that makes storing more than one version of a prompt possible, and the documented "Prompt Engine renders, caller invokes the LLM Gateway" chain is now proven end to end with a real provider and a real `Agent` caller, not just `PromptedCompletionService`'s own standalone composition; (e) a multi-instance worker loop with scheduling/polling, a decision for how often to invoke the reaper, and mid-step renewal decisions; (f) a real Human Approval Point execution path that actually writes to `approvals` — still deliberately not attempted, since nothing in the roadmap requires more than one instance yet and no step-level contract exists yet to trigger it from; (g) the Context Manager now has a real first slice plus a real Size & Token Budget Enforcer — `ContextManager`/`ContextSourceResolver` Protocols, one real `WorkflowStateResolver`, a real `AgentStepExecutor`/`PromptedAgent` wiring, and now genuine token-budget admission (rank by `relevance_score`, a stable sort, greedily admit what fits, report `items_excluded_count` honestly) — all proven end to end, including real-database integration tests showing both a workflow's own `inputs` genuinely reaching a rendered prompt and a real oversized item genuinely being excluded. implementation_roadmap.md §3's own three-part Stage B deliverable for this component ("deterministic assembly, budget enforcement, and trust tagging") is therefore now fully met — but only one of context_manager.md §3's six documented sources is built; Knowledge Manager, Memory Manager, AI Context Packs, and a Runtime Configuration resolver remain unbuilt or deferred, and a real Filter/Ranker as a first-class capability and a Context Audit Logger persistence writer still do not exist (see §3's own Context Manager entries and §5).

---

## 5. Blockers / Risks

- **CI has still never actually executed on GitHub's servers** — this working tree is not yet a git repository (no remote, no pushes, nothing to trigger a workflow run). `.github/workflows/ci.yml` is now aligned with the codebase and every real (non-gated) command in it has been verified locally, command-for-command (see §3), but that is not the same guarantee as a real Actions run. Re-verify once this becomes a pushed git repository with Actions enabled.
- **Only the `env` secrets backend exists.** `AnthropicAdapter` now resolves its API key through `EnvSecretProvider` (ADR-0024 explicitly scopes this backend to local development only) — real production use still needs Vault or a cloud secret manager, neither of which exist yet. Not a blocker for this step (local development and the opt-in live test both work today), but a real blocker before any production deployment calls a real provider.
- **`uv` is still not on `PATH` in this Windows session** (unchanged from earlier steps — `.venv/Scripts/{python,pytest,mypy,ruff}.exe` are used directly). Adding the `anthropic` dependency required regenerating `uv.lock`; `uv` was installed via `pip install uv` into the project venv (not the system binary) to do this. `uv sync --frozen --all-packages` was then verified twice: once with the latest `uv` (0.11.32), and once with `uv==0.5.11` — the exact version `ci.yml` pins (`UV_VERSION: "0.5.11"`) — confirming the regenerated lockfile is genuinely compatible with the CI-pinned CLI, not just a newer one.
- **Environment note, not a repo defect:** the development machine has a native (non-Docker) Postgres already listening on `0.0.0.0:5432`, which shadows the compose container's default port mapping for host-side connections. `infra/docker-compose.yml` already supports overriding via `AIOS_DB_PORT` (used `5434` during verification); worth remembering if `localhost:5432` ever appears to "hang" or reject credentials that are actually correct.
- **`__pycache__` directories present under `kernel/src/`** — harmless (covered by `.gitignore`), noted only because this is not yet a git repository, so nothing has been committed or excluded yet.
- **Bearer tokens are pre-shared-secret JWTs, not full OIDC.** `JWTBearerTokenVerifier` (see §3) verifies signature and expiry against an `AIOS_SECRET_SECURITY_JWT_SIGNING_KEY` shared secret — there is no identity provider, no JWKS endpoint, no issuer/audience check. Fine for local development and for proving the auth boundary genuinely works (this step's own tests mint tokens with the same shared secret), but **not production-credible as-is**: anyone holding that one shared secret can mint a token for any principal/role. Replacing it with a real OIDC-backed `TokenVerifier` is the documented upgrade path (§3) and does not require changing any caller — but it has not happened yet, and must before any non-development deployment exposes this route.
- **Nine routes exist, and all are narrower than their own documented (or, for `POST /api/v1/packs`, entirely undocumented) contracts.** `POST /api/v1/workflows` accepts only `inputs` and returns `200` with a reduced body instead of api_architecture.md §6.1's `202`/`definition_id`/`Idempotency-Key` shape; `GET /api/v1/workflows` has no filtering; `/steps`/`.../events` are not paginated; `POST /api/v1/packs` is not in §6.5's documented list at all; pack activate/deactivate return `200` not §6.5's documented `202`; there is no `GET /api/v1/packs` (list). Every other §6 endpoint (approvals, experiments, gates, usage, agents, config, traceability, `/stream`) still returns 404 (FastAPI's default for an undefined route).
- **Pack activation still has no ADR-0007 human-approval gating.** capability_manager.md §4 and api_architecture.md §6.5 both note that activation of a pack affecting platform behaviour requires human approval; `POST /api/v1/packs/{id}/activate` activates immediately and synchronously for anyone holding `pack:manage`, with no approval step at all. Acceptable for this minimal slice (explicitly out of scope — "no permissions matrix" — and no real pack yet has meaningfully "platform-affecting" behaviour to gate), but a real blocker before this route is exposed to anyone beyond a trusted operator.
- **A second real, network-calling provider adapter now exists (`LocalAdapter`), but is not registered by default in the checked-in production config.** `config/llm.yaml`'s `local_provider` section is commented out — no local model server is assumed running in a fresh checkout — so `kernel/bootstrap.py` registers only `anthropic` today. Genuine two-real-provider dispatch is proven by `test_multi_provider_routing.py` against real local HTTP servers standing in for both APIs, not (yet) by a live call to a real Ollama/vLLM/LM Studio instance from the running Kernel.
- **The real fallback chain that exists today is same-provider only; the cross-provider example is commented out for the same reason as `local_provider`.** `coding-strong` genuinely falls back from Anthropic to Anthropic (`claude-opus-5` -> `claude-sonnet-5`) — real chain traversal, proven by a real composition-root test, but it does not exercise a cross-provider failure path against the live Kernel. Fallback itself is still ungated by the Error Taxonomy on purpose (any `LLMProviderError` triggers a fallback attempt, retriable or not — a different provider is a different call, see §3) — only same-candidate retry and circuit-breaker counting are gated by classification.
- **The Circuit Breaker's, the backoff policy's, and both budget enforcers' numbers are hardcoded constants in `kernel/bootstrap.py`, not configuration.** `_CIRCUIT_BREAKER_FAILURE_THRESHOLD = 5`/`_CIRCUIT_BREAKER_RESET_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 30.0`, `_BACKOFF_MAX_ATTEMPTS = 3`/`_BACKOFF_BASE_DELAY_SECONDS = 0.5`/`_BACKOFF_MAX_DELAY_SECONDS = 8.0`/`_BACKOFF_MAX_TOTAL_SECONDS = 15.0`, and `_ALIAS_BUDGET_CEILING_USD = Decimal("10.00")`/`_WORKFLOW_BUDGET_CEILING_USD = Decimal("25.00")` are all real, documented, but not tunable without a code change and redeploy — the identical, already-accepted carve-out `_PROMPTED_AGENT_MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS` uses, not a new gap this step introduces. The Circuit Breaker and both budget enforcers are also all **not persisted across process restarts** — a multi-instance or frequently-restarted deployment would lose all circuit state and all recorded spend on every restart, always starting every provider `CLOSED` and every alias/workflow at zero spend again; a real, plausible second implementation of either (e.g. Redis-backed) is deferred, not yet justified by a second real need (ADR-0004).
- **`Retry-After`'s HTTP-date form is not parsed.** `parse_retry_after_seconds()` reads only the numeric-seconds form (the common case for rate limiting); a provider sending `Retry-After: Wed, 21 Oct 2026 07:28:00 GMT` degrades to this Gateway's own computed exponential-backoff delay instead — a real, documented gap, not a crash. The platform-wide `AiOsError` hierarchy `error_handling_retry.md` §8 describes also still does not exist anywhere in the codebase — this step's own `LLMProviderError`/`LLMRefusalError` fields use that document's vocabulary and field names so a future adoption is a migration, not a rewrite, but the hierarchy itself remains unbuilt, deliberately out of scope for an LLM-Gateway-encapsulated step.
- **Two of llm_gateway.md §9's five documented policy/budget checks are now real (per-alias and per-workflow cost ceilings); per-step and per-experiment budgets, allowed-model policy, per-principal rate limiting, and context-window-fit pre-checks remain entirely unbuilt.** Per-step is blocked on the Step Contract itself (workflow_architecture.md documents no budget field on a step); per-experiment is blocked on the Benchmarking Pack, which does not exist yet (evaluation_engine.md §5.1: experiment definition lives in that pack, not the Evaluation Engine); allowed-model policy needs a `security` `ErrorCategory` this project's taxonomy deliberately excludes today; per-principal rate limiting needs principal propagation that stops at `WorkflowInstance.principal_id` today and never reaches the LLM Gateway; context-window fit now has a real `ProviderCapabilities.max_input_tokens` to check against (see §3's Capability Negotiator entry), but still needs `count_tokens()` (llm_gateway.md §12 — a real provider-endpoint token count), which does not exist, so the check itself remains unbuilt. No pre-flight cost estimate for either real ceiling — a real call's cost can push cumulative spend past its ceiling before the *next* call is refused, since nothing here guesses an upcoming cost (§12 forbids approximating a token count from anything but a provider's own endpoint).
- **The Capability Negotiator has a real matrix and lookup, but none of llm_gateway.md §6's four documented degradation rules (use/emulate/fail/never-emulate) exist, and its numeric/boolean values are unverified against live provider documentation.** `DispatchingLLMGateway.capabilities(alias)` answers a real question, but nothing calls it — no tool-calling, structured-output emulation, or streaming exists to consult it, and `LLMRequest` has no `require_capabilities` field for rule 4 to check. `config/llm.yaml`'s new `capabilities:` section is the author's best available knowledge, explicitly flagged in the file's own header comment as not machine-verified — an operator should confirm each value against the provider's current documentation before any future degradation logic relies on it for a safety decision.
- **platform_sdk.md §5.1's `ProviderCapabilities` listing (ten fields) and llm_gateway.md §6's own listing (thirteen fields) disagree, with no stated precedence between them for this shape.** This step implemented the fuller, thirteen-field llm_gateway.md version (see §3), a reasoned choice, not a resolved one — platform_sdk.md should be reconciled with it in a future documentation pass.
- **`TraceContext` does not yet carry `trace_id`/`span_id`, even though real values already exist for both today** (this Kernel's own OpenTelemetry span instrumentation, ADR-0017). Wiring the active span's own trace/span id into `TraceContext` is a distinct, later Observability-integration step, not attempted here, to keep this step scoped to Policy & Budget. Also absent: `agent_id`, `experiment_id`, `run_id` — this step's own approved exclusions.
- **platform_sdk.md §4.2's `AgentRequest` documents `workflow_id`/`step_id` both as top-level fields and nested inside its own `trace: TraceContext`** — a real redundancy in the documentation itself, surfaced while implementing `TraceContext` for the first time, not resolved here since `AgentRequest` is untouched by this step. Worth clarifying in platform_sdk.md before any component implements `AgentRequest` for real.
- **The Context Manager has exactly one real resolver (Workflow State); Knowledge Manager, Memory Manager, AI Context Packs, and Runtime Configuration are all still absent.** context_manager.md §3 documents six sources; only one is built. Every assembled context today can only ever contain a workflow's own declared `inputs` — no retrieved documentation, no engineering memory, no curated context packages, no configuration values. A real coding/architecture agent needs at least Knowledge Manager integration to be useful for anything beyond an echo-style demo.
- **Budget enforcement is now real, but there is still no filtering or ranking as first-class capabilities in the Context Manager.** `relevance_score` is still a constant `1.0` for every item (no ranking model) — the Size & Token Budget Enforcer reuses it only as a stable truncation tie-break, not as genuine ranking. A resolver returning many items today would be truncated to fit `_CONTEXT_TOKEN_BUDGET`, but *which* items survive is still arbitrary beyond "resolver order" until a real Filter/Ranker exists — a real risk once Knowledge/Memory resolvers exist and truncation choices start to matter qualitatively, not just quantitatively.
- **No persistence schema exists for a context assembly at all.** context_manager.md §9 documents a "Context Audit Logger" requirement ("every context assembly must record ... for audit and replay"), but data_model.md defines no table for one. `AssembledContext` is purely in-memory, for the duration of one request, with `assembly_id` naming an object that cannot currently be looked back up. A schema needs to be designed and documented before a writer can be built.
- **context_manager.md §6 does not state whether workflow/user-provided input is `trusted` or `untrusted`.** `WorkflowStateResolver` defaults to `untrusted` (reasoned: it originates outside the Kernel's own trusted subsystems), but this is this step's own judgment call, not a documented certainty — worth an explicit statement in a future revision of context_manager.md.
- **Environment note, not a repo defect:** Docker Desktop was not running in this session's environment when the full test suite (including real-Postgres integration tests via testcontainers) was run after this step's changes — every integration test errored on `docker.errors.DockerException` (`CreateFile`/named-pipe failure), not a code regression. The full unit suite (497 tests, including all new Size & Token Budget Enforcer tests) passed cleanly on its own. The two new/modified real-database tests in `test_bootstrap_workflow_trigger.py` (including this step's own new budget-exclusion proof) were verified by mypy/ruff and by hand-tracing the shared admission algorithm against the already-passing unit tests, but were not re-executed against a real container this session — re-run once Docker Desktop is available.
- **Environment note (continued):** Docker Desktop was still unavailable during the Capability Negotiator step (see §3) — the unit suite (521 tests, all new Capability Negotiator tests included, 100% coverage on every touched file) passed cleanly on its own; the real-database integration suite has now gone unverified for two consecutive steps and should be re-run in full once Docker Desktop is available again, since neither step's own changes can be fully confirmed against a real Postgres instance without it.
- **Environment note (continued, three consecutive steps now):** Docker Desktop was still unavailable during the Knowledge Schema step (see §3) — the 14 new integration tests (including the `pg_extension` check, the generated `content_tsv` proof, and the varying-vector-dimension proof) have never been executed against a real Postgres/pgvector instance; verification instead relied on direct DDL compilation (`CreateTable(...).compile(dialect=postgresql.dialect())`) plus `mypy --strict`/`ruff`. This is now the single highest-priority re-verification item once Docker Desktop is available again — it is the first step in this project to introduce a genuinely new Postgres extension (`pgvector`) and a generated column backed by it, neither of which DDL compilation alone can prove actually works against a real server.
- **`retrieval.md` does not exist anywhere in the docs tree, despite being the name this project's own process referred to when scoping this step.** The real, governing document is `docs/03_architecture/services/search_vector_search.md` (confirmed via `DOCUMENTATION_INDEX.md` §9). Not a blocker to this step (the correct document was found and used), but worth a documentation pass — either renaming the real file or adding a redirect/alias note — so the discrepancy does not recur in a future Retrieval step.
- **`embeddings.embedding` has no fixed dimension and its documented HNSW index does not exist yet — both pending a real embedding-model decision this codebase has not made.** data_model.md §7 documents "HNSW on `embeddings.embedding` (cosine)" as if a dimension were already settled; it is not. Any future step that starts generating real embeddings (via the LLM Gateway's still-unbuilt `embed()`) must also choose a concrete embedding model and dimension at that point, then add the HNSW index in a purely additive migration — this is a real, named, currently-open architectural decision, not an oversight of this step.
- **ADR-0011's documented "SQLite through the same repository interfaces" local-development mode is not implemented anywhere in this codebase, for any table, `knowledge` included.** A pre-existing, project-wide documentation-vs-implementation gap (every schema module targets Postgres/`asyncpg` directly), re-confirmed rather than newly introduced by this step; noted here since it applies equally to the new `knowledge` schema.
- **Environment note (continued, four consecutive steps now):** Docker Desktop was still unavailable during the `knowledge.documents`/`knowledge.chunks` writer step (see §3) — the 10 new integration tests have never executed against a real Postgres/pgvector instance; verification instead relied on compiling the writer's own `INSERT` statements against the PostgreSQL dialect (confirming the generated `content_tsv` column is correctly excluded from the compiled column list for the writer's own executemany-style call) and directly exercising the writer's pure-Python validation/`ordinal`-derivation logic against a stub engine. Re-running the full integration suite (now covering both the Knowledge Schema and this writer) is the single highest-priority re-verification item once Docker Desktop is available again — four consecutive steps have shipped with no real-database confirmation.
- **The `knowledge.documents`/`knowledge.chunks` writer has no consumer.** No Indexing Pipeline, ingestion path, Knowledge Manager, or API route calls `SqlKnowledgeWriter` anywhere in this codebase yet — it is proven only by its own integration tests, the same "real but not yet exercised end to end by the running Kernel" position `SqlAgentRegistry`/`SqlToolRegistry` were in before `PromptedAgent` used them for real. `kernel/bootstrap.py` deliberately does not construct one, since there is nothing yet to wire it into.
- **Deduplication of re-ingested content is an open policy question, not decided.** `SqlKnowledgeWriter.write_document()` always creates a new `document_id`, even when called twice with byte-identical `content_hash`/chunks — whether a future Indexing Pipeline should detect and skip/update a duplicate, or intentionally allow re-ingestion to create a new version, is left for that future step to decide; nothing in data_model.md §7 or search_vector_search.md states a rule for this today.
- **Environment note (continued, five consecutive steps now):** Docker Desktop was still unavailable during the Keyword Search reader step (see §3) — the 10 new integration tests have never executed against a real Postgres/pgvector instance; verification instead relied on compiling the reader's exact `SELECT`/`WHERE`/`ORDER BY` statement against the PostgreSQL dialect, confirming valid `ts_rank(...)`/`plainto_tsquery(...)`/`@@` SQL with the expected bind parameters. Five consecutive steps have now shipped with no real-database confirmation; re-running the full integration suite (schema, writer, and reader together) is the single highest-priority re-verification item once Docker Desktop is available again — this reader in particular depends on genuine Postgres full-text-search runtime behaviour (stemming, ranking) that no amount of SQL-compilation checking can fully prove.
- **The Keyword Search reader has no consumer and no HTTP surface.** No Context Manager resolver, Knowledge Manager, or API route calls `SqlKeywordSearcher` anywhere in this codebase yet — proven only by its own integration tests. `kernel/bootstrap.py` deliberately does not construct one, mirroring the writer's own "no consumer yet" position.
- **Keyword search alone cannot see `trust`/`project_id`.** `ChunkSearchResult` does not carry either field (not named in this step's own approved four-field result shape) — a future consumer wanting to filter by trust level or scope to a project cannot do so from this reader's own output alone; it would need to join back to `knowledge.documents` itself. Not a defect (out of scope, per search_vector_search.md's own separate "Metadata Filter"/"Access Control" components), but worth remembering before any real consumer is built.
- **`LocalSubprocessSandbox` must never be used to execute genuinely untrusted or hostile code — it does not satisfy ADR-0016 Tier 1, by the ADR's own explicit words.** ADR-0016's "Alternatives Considered" rejects plain subprocess execution outright as offering "no meaningful containment for hostile code"; this backend does not change that. Its own `SandboxGuarantees` declares `enforces_network_isolation=False`/`enforces_filesystem_containment=False` precisely so no future caller can mistake it for the real thing. **Partially resolved (§3): a real `DockerSandbox`/OCI backend now exists and genuinely satisfies ADR-0016 Tier 1** — but `LocalSubprocessSandbox` remains every agent's own default (no agent/pack code was changed), so this warning still applies to every real, running instance of this pipeline today. Switching the default is the explicitly-recommended next step (§6), blocked on the `sys.executable`-portability gap recorded below.
- **No memory/CPU/PID limits are enforced by `LocalSubprocessSandbox`.** ADR-0016 assigns these to OCI runtime configuration; a bare subprocess needs platform-specific primitives (POSIX `resource.setrlimit`, Windows Job Objects) this step did not implement, judged real, avoidable complexity for a first slice. A command that spawns unbounded memory or child processes is not currently contained by anything in this codebase. **Resolved for `DockerSandbox` (§3)**: `mem_limit`/`nano_cpus`/`pids_limit` are real, enforced cgroup limits there — this entry still applies only to `LocalSubprocessSandbox` itself, unchanged and still the default.
- **`DockerSandbox` exists and genuinely satisfies ADR-0016 Tier 1, but no agent's own commands are actually portable to it yet — a real, discovered, load-bearing gap.** `BuildAgentEntrypoint`/`TestAgentEntrypoint`/`DocumentationAgentEntrypoint` all construct `command` sequences using `sys.executable`, the *host's own* absolute interpreter path — meaningless inside `DockerSandbox`'s own minimal container image, which has no file at that path. Running any of this pack's real, already-shipped commands against `DockerSandbox` unmodified would fail with "no such file or directory." Resolving this needs either a documented, portable interpreter-invocation convention (e.g., agents constructing `["python3", ...]` and relying on the sandbox backend's own image to provide it on `PATH`, rather than resolving `sys.executable` at all) or a per-backend command-adaptation seam — real design work, explicitly out of this step's own scope ("do not change any agent, pack, or Workflow Engine code"), and the single hard prerequisite before `DockerSandbox` can become any agent's own default.
- **This project's own "real fakes, never mocks" testing convention now has one deliberate, recorded exception.** `tests/unit/kernel/sandbox/test_docker_executor.py` uses `unittest.mock` against the third-party `docker` SDK — narrowly scoped to call-construction/timeout/capping logic, never to the guarantees a real daemon alone can prove (covered instead by the real, currently-skipped `tests/integration/sandbox/test_docker_sandbox_live.py`). Worth remembering as precedent should a future step need to wrap another third-party, unownable client library this project cannot meaningfully fake in-process.
- **The default sandbox image (`python:3.12-slim`) is tag-pinned, not digest-pinned** — security_architecture.md §10 names "pinned by digest" as the real target for sandbox images. Deferred as a real, later hardening step (the identical treatment ADR-0016 itself already gives gVisor), not attempted here since no specific production image has been chosen yet to pin.
- **`DockerSandbox`'s own stdin-delivery path (`docker`'s raw `attach_socket` API) is this backend's single most platform/version-sensitive mechanism, and could not be exercised against a real daemon this session.** The unit tests confirm the *call sequence* is correct against a mocked client; only `tests/integration/sandbox/test_docker_sandbox_live.py::test_stdin_delivery_genuinely_reaches_the_container` (currently skipped — Docker unavailable) can confirm it actually works against a real container. The single most important individual test to re-run first once Docker Desktop is available, given how much of this pack's own Build/Documentation agents depend on stdin-based content delivery.
- **`DockerSandbox`'s own output-cap enforcement is a client-side, post-hoc truncation, not the live, eager-kill-the-instant-the-cap-is-hit draining loop `LocalSubprocessSandbox` uses.** The caller-visible outcome is identical (never more than `max_output_bytes` per stream), and the wall-clock timeout remains a hard backstop regardless, but a container producing an extreme volume of output before either finishing or timing out is not killed early by the cap alone the way a bare subprocess is — recorded as an honest mechanical difference, not a guarantee gap (`enforces_output_cap=True` is still accurate for the outcome it actually promises).
- **This codebase has no `tool_invoker` package at all — not even an empty stub**, unlike `knowledge_manager`/`memory_manager`/`retrieval` (confirmed by direct inspection during this step). Worth creating as an explicit, documented placeholder in a near-future step, the same "record the gap, don't silently fill it" treatment already given to other confirmed-absent components.
- **Environment note:** a real, Windows-`ProactorEventLoop`-specific asyncio bug was found and fixed during this step (see §3) — cancelling a task blocked on a subprocess pipe read does not reliably unblock on Windows; the fix kills the process before cancelling. This was caught only by actually running the tests, not by `mypy`/`ruff`/inspection — a reminder that this session's Windows environment, while normally just an inconvenience for Docker-dependent steps, can also surface real, CI-relevant platform bugs that a Linux-only CI run might not have caught as early. A cosmetic, non-blocking `ResourceWarning` ("unclosed transport"/"Event loop is closed") is still observed on Windows when a killed subprocess's pipes are torn down after the per-test event loop closes; not reproduced by inspection as a Linux/CI concern, not chased further this step (see §3).
- **`SandboxedCommandTool` genuinely dispatches, but still through `LocalSubprocessSandbox` — the "must never be used for untrusted/hostile code" warning above applies with exactly the same force to this Tool.** Nothing about a real `Tool` calling a real `SandboxExecutor` upgrades the underlying isolation; a `DockerSandbox`/OCI backend remains the hard prerequisite before this dispatch path is exercised against LLM-generated code from an untrusted or ingested-repository source. This is stated in three places now (the Sandbox Executor's own docstring, `SandboxedCommandTool`'s own docstring, and here) deliberately, so it cannot be missed by only reading one of them.
- **A `SandboxedCommandTool`'s command is fixed at construction, not driven by any per-step declaration.** Until a real `input_schema`/per-step-input mechanism exists (still deferred, unchanged since this Tool contract was first reduced), every real use of this Tool needs its own dedicated construction site (in a test, or eventually a Capability Pack's own entrypoint wiring) — there is no way for a workflow definition to say *which* command a tool step should run.
- **This codebase still has no `tool_invoker` package at all** (re-confirmed by inspection this step, first recorded last step) — worth creating as an explicit, documented empty stub in a near-future step, the same treatment `knowledge_manager`/`memory_manager`/`retrieval` already have.
- **No real workflow (via `kernel/bootstrap.py` or an HTTP route) has ever executed a `SandboxedCommandTool` directly as a declared Tool.** The Build Agent now genuinely uses it (see §3), but by internal composition, not as a manifest-declared, `ToolStepExecutor`-dispatched Tool of its own — no `WorkflowStep` of type `tool` naming a `SandboxedCommandTool` has ever run through the real `kernel/bootstrap.py`/HTTP path either.
- **No automated pipeline turns a registered pack's manifest into real `catalog.agents`/`catalog.tools` rows, and nothing calls a pack's own `CapabilityPack.activate()`.** `SqlPackLifecycleRepository.register()` only ever writes `catalog.packs`/`catalog.pack_state_transitions` — parsing `manifest.agents[]`/`tools[]` and writing the corresponding catalog rows automatically is a real, distinct, larger Capability Manager increment (a real pack installer) that does not exist. Every agent/tool row in this codebase's history, including this step's own, has been seeded directly by a test — this is consistent with prior steps, not a new gap this one introduces, but it is now more visible with a real pack in the repository.
- **`ai_os_kernel.capability_manager.pack_contract`'s `PackContext`/`PackRegistration`/`HealthReport` are a deliberately reduced slice of platform_sdk.md §6/§7 — not a faithful implementation of the full documented shape.** `PackContext` carries only `pack_id`/`pack_version`; the full documented version names fourteen optional services (LLM Gateway, Prompt Registry, Context Service, Retrieval, Memory, a real Tool Invoker, Event Bus, Config Service, Secret Resolver, Storage, Workspace, Telemetry, Traceability, Quality Gate Registry, Speech Gateway), almost none of which exist as real Kernel components. A future pack needing any of these will need this module extended additively, not redesigned — the identical position `TraceContext`/`ContextRequest` were in before their own later real uses arrived.
- **This pack imports Kernel internals directly — a known, documented, temporary architectural compromise, not a pattern for future packs to copy without re-examining whether it still applies.** capability_pack_contract.md's own "Platform Interaction Rules" prohibit direct Kernel access; no `ai-os-sdk` package exists yet for a pack to depend on instead. Both `agents/architecture.py`'s own docstring and the pack's own `pyproject.toml` record this explicitly. Resolving it is Platform SDK v1.0.0's own migration to make.
- **The Architecture Agent's own prompt (`prompts/architecture_proposal.md`) is never actually loaded by anything yet.** No automated "load a pack's shipped prompt file into `catalog.prompts`" mechanism exists (matching Discovery 2 above) — this step's own opt-in live test seeds a different, self-contained, test-only prompt instead, specifically because the real prompt's `{{context}}` placeholder needs a real Context Manager/workflow-instance round trip this focused proof does not stand up. A future step wiring this pack into a real workflow needs to either build that automated loader or seed the real prompt content the same way this step's tests seed everything else.
- **Environment note (continued, this session's Docker-unavailable steps now numbering more than can be individually listed):** Docker Desktop was unavailable again during this step — `tests/integration/workflow_engine/test_architecture_agent_pack.py` (both its deterministic and opt-in-live tiers) could not be executed live. Verified instead via `pytest --collect-only` (the file imports and collects cleanly) plus manual, direct execution of the same underlying operations the test performs (`ManifestLoader.load_one()` against the real manifest, `EntrypointLoader.load()` against both real entrypoint strings) — these are the same real code paths the integration test itself exercises, just not run through pytest/testcontainers this session.
- **The Build Agent's own working directory has no real per-workflow identity — each agent instance gets one private temp directory, not the documented per-workflow workspace (security_architecture.md §5.3, still Stage C).** A Test Agent verifying what the Build Agent wrote needs the new `workingDirectory` output field to find it; a real Workspace Service, once built, replaces this default without changing the agent's own write path.
- **The Build Agent's own prompt (`prompts/build_write_file.md`) is never actually loaded by anything yet** — the identical gap already recorded for the Architecture Agent's own prompt (previous step), for the identical reason (no automated pack-prompt-loader exists, and the real prompt's `{{context}}` placeholder needs a Context Manager round trip this step's own live test does not stand up).
- **No automatic Architecture -> Build pipeline exists, and this step's own approved framing explicitly deferred it.** The Architecture Agent's real output has never been fed into the Build Agent's real input by any code in this codebase — both agents are proven independently. Wiring one's output into the other (even manually, without a full Workflow Engine multi-step definition) is a distinct, real, not-yet-attempted increment.
- **`SandboxExecutor`'s new `stdin` parameter is delivered best-effort, with no cap of its own.** Unlike `max_output_bytes` (which bounds what is *read back*), nothing bounds how much can be *written* via `stdin` — a caller supplying an enormous byte string would still be handed to the subprocess in full. Not a concern for this step's own use (one generated file, realistically well under any reasonable limit), but worth a bound if a future caller's input size is less predictable.
- **Environment note (continued):** Docker Desktop was unavailable again during this step — `tests/integration/workflow_engine/test_build_agent_pack.py` could not be executed live. Verified instead via `pytest --collect-only` plus direct execution of every unit-testable real code path (`stdin` delivery, the parser, the path validator, and the full deterministic dispatch chain via `InMemoryAgentRegistry`) — the same underlying operations the integration test itself exercises.
- **`AgentStepExecutor` has no per-step mechanism for arbitrary structured input beyond `promptId`/`promptVersion`/`modelAlias`/`context` — a real, discovered gap the Test Agent's own `_extract_payload()` works around, not resolves.** Every non-`PromptedAgent` agent needing more than one named structured value (the Test Agent's `workingDirectory`/`filePath`/`runCommand`) must, today, either receive them directly in `inputs` (only possible when called outside the real `AgentStepExecutor` dispatch path) or encode them as JSON inside the one real `context` channel — a workaround, not a first-class per-step input mechanism. A real fix (a genuine per-step `inputs`/parameters field on the Step Contract, validated against an `input_schema`) remains unbuilt and would let a future agent avoid this JSON-in-context encoding entirely.
- **The manifest schema (`platform_sdk/schemas/manifest.schema.json`) requires `modelAlias` on every `agents[]` entry unconditionally, with no escape hatch for an agent that calls no model.** The Test Agent's own manifest entry declares a syntactically-valid but genuinely unused `modelAlias: coding-strong` to satisfy the schema (see §3) — a real, recorded schema/implementation mismatch. Any future non-`PromptedAgent`-backed agent will hit the identical requirement; fixing it means making `modelAlias` conditional in the schema itself (real, later Platform SDK work, out of scope for any agent-level step).
- **The Test Agent's own `Test`-prefixed class names (`TestAgentEntrypoint`, `TestAgentInput`, `TestAgentOutput`, `TestInstructionError`) trigger a cosmetic `PytestCollectionWarning` whenever imported into any `test_*.py` file** — pytest scans every test module's namespace for `Test*`-prefixed names regardless of where they are actually defined, and each of these declares an `__init__`, so pytest auto-skips them (nothing is ever mis-collected as a real test). Deliberately not renamed, to preserve the `Architecture`/`Build`/`Test` naming symmetry this pack's own manifest and code already share; accepted as a known, harmless, documented warning rather than "fixed."
- **Environment note (continued):** Docker Desktop was unavailable again during this step — `tests/integration/workflow_engine/test_verification_agent_pack.py` could not be executed live. Verified instead via `pytest --collect-only` plus direct execution of every unit-testable real code path (both dispatch outcomes via `AgentStepExecutor`, the payload extractor, the path validator) — the same underlying operations the integration test itself exercises. This agent's own tests needed no live-provider-key workaround at all (see §3) — only the real-Postgres-backed `SqlAgentRegistry` resolution tier is affected.
- **Resolved this session (§3): Architecture -> Build -> Test -> Documentation now chain through one real, declared `WorkflowDefinition`** (`se.delivery_pipeline`), via the new `WorkflowStepOutputResolver`. This entry is kept here, marked resolved, rather than deleted, so this document's own history of the gap remains traceable.
- **The Documentation Agent's own documentation artifact is a plain Markdown file written through the sandbox, not a `knowledge.documents` row or any other queryable persistence target.** A future consumer wanting to search across many generated documentation files (rather than reading one specific `<filePath>.md` a human or another agent already knows the location of) would need a real ingestion path into `knowledge.documents`/`knowledge.chunks` (§4's own Retrieval row) — deliberately out of scope for this step, which named "write it as a real file via the sandbox" explicitly.
- **The Documentation Agent's own prompt (`prompts/documentation_record_artifact.md`) is loaded and used for real by this step's own opt-in live integration test — a genuine, positive difference from Architecture's/Build's own prompts, which remain never actually loaded by anything outside their own test-only substitutes (see the two entries above, prior steps).** This works specifically because this agent's structured input reaches it via direct `inputs` keys, not a Context Manager round trip; it does not resolve the underlying gap those two agents still have (no automated "load a pack's shipped prompt file into `catalog.prompts`" mechanism exists for any of them in production — every live test in this pack, including this one, still seeds `catalog.prompts` directly via raw SQL).
- **Environment note (continued):** Docker Desktop was unavailable again during this step — `tests/integration/workflow_engine/test_documentation_agent_pack.py` could not be executed live. Verified instead via `pytest --collect-only` plus direct execution of every unit-testable real code path (both dispatch outcomes via `AgentStepExecutor`, the payload extractor, both path validators) — the same underlying operations the integration test itself exercises. One unrelated, pre-existing, timing-sensitive test (`test_local_adapter.py::test_complete_honours_a_real_retry_after_header`) flaked once under full-suite load this session and passed cleanly in isolation — noted here since it surfaced during this step's own verification sweep, not because this step's changes are implicated.
- **Resolved (2026-07-28, see §3's own reconciliation-step section): The real, Postgres-backed proof of the four-step pipeline (`tests/integration/workflow_engine/test_delivery_pipeline.py`) has now genuinely executed — its deterministic tier passed for real.** Originally recorded here: "has never executed this session — Docker Desktop was unavailable again... the single highest-priority re-verification item in this entire document." Docker Desktop became available two steps later; the deterministic tier now passes as part of a full, 218-passed real integration run. The opt-in live tier remains correctly skipped (no `AIOS_SECRET_LLM_ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` configured) — that is expected, opt-in behaviour, not an open gap.
- **`WorkflowStateResolver`, unmodified and still correct for every existing single-step caller, silently corrupts a multi-resolver, multi-step assembly if a caller does not know to scope it.** This is a real, generalizable trap for a *future* pipeline author, not fully closed by this step's own narrow `_StepScopedResolver` fix (which lives in this one pipeline's own composition code, not the Kernel): any future `DefaultContextManager` combining `WorkflowStateResolver` with any resolver expecting a single clean JSON/text payload for a non-first step will hit the identical corruption unless it also remembers to scope `WorkflowStateResolver` itself. Worth a documented convention, or a Kernel-level fix (e.g., `WorkflowStateResolver` gaining an optional step-scoping parameter of its own), before a second real pipeline is built — not attempted here, since one narrow, working fix was enough to prove this step's own approved scope.
- **`_StepScopedResolver` is pipeline-owned, ad-hoc glue, not a documented, reusable Kernel capability** — a real, generic "restrict any resolver to specific steps" wrapper would plausibly belong in `ai_os_kernel.context_manager` itself (sibling to `WorkflowStepOutputResolver`), reusable by any future multi-step pipeline; deferred rather than built speculatively for a still-hypothetical second pipeline (ADR-0004).
- **The one enrichment this pipeline needs (`runCommand = [sys.executable, filePath]`) encodes a real, narrow assumption — this pipeline's own Architecture step produces a Python file — that is recorded in exactly one place (`pipeline.py`'s own `_run_generated_file_with_python`) and nowhere enforced.** A future requirement asking for a non-Python file would silently produce a `runCommand` that fails to run it; nothing here validates the assumption before acting on it. Acceptable for this step's own single, fixed, Python-oriented proof; a real, general "how do I run this file" mechanism (language detection, a declared runner per file type) is a distinct, larger, later increment.
- **`catalog.workflow_definitions` has no automated installer either — this step's own new manifest `workflows[]` entry is schema-valid but, like every `agents[]`/`prompts[]` entry before it, is never automatically turned into a real catalog row.** `WorkflowInstanceService.create_instance()` calls `WorkflowDefinitionCatalog.register()` itself at instance-creation time (an existing, real, already-built mechanism — unlike agents/prompts, a workflow definition genuinely does get written to `catalog.workflow_definitions` the first time it is actually triggered), but nothing scans a pack's own manifest to register a workflow *before* it is first run, and nothing populates `catalog.packs`' own knowledge that this pack declares one at all.
- **Resolved (2026-07-28, see §3's own reconciliation-step section): `tests/integration/sandbox/test_docker_sandbox_live.py`'s own 7 tests now genuinely executed, all passing.** Originally recorded here as collected-but-skipped with Docker unavailable. Docker Desktop became available during the reconciliation step; the resulting real run found and fixed two genuine bugs (missing image auto-pull; a Windows/npipe stdin-EOF bug fixed via `detach=False`) before all 7 passed for real.
- **Closing note on this document's own long-running "Docker Desktop was unavailable" thread (2026-07-28).** Every entry above recording "Docker Desktop was unavailable this session" was accurate at the time it was written — this document does not rewrite them. As of the reconciliation step (see §3), Docker Desktop became available in this development environment for the first time in this project's history, and a full real run of `tests/integration` (not `--collect-only`, not a manual trace) reported **218 passed, 10 correctly-skipped, 0 failed** — closing the re-verification debt every one of the entries above was tracking. This does not guarantee Docker remains available in every future session; if it becomes unavailable again, the same accurate "unavailable this session" recording convention resumes rather than being treated as a regression.

---

## 6. Recommended Next Small Step

**Unchanged from the previous step, and now stronger: wire `DockerSandbox` in as the pipeline's real sandbox backend.** This step was documentation reconciliation and verification-debt closure, not development — it deliberately did not touch this recommendation's own substance. What has changed is confidence: `DockerSandbox` is no longer merely "real and tested against mocks" — this step's own real run against a genuine daemon found and fixed two real bugs (missing image auto-pull; the Windows/npipe stdin-EOF issue) and then proved all 7 of its own Tier 1 guarantees for real, unmocked, against a live container. The one remaining blocker is unchanged and still real: this pack's own agents construct commands using `sys.executable`, a host-specific path that does not exist inside `DockerSandbox`'s own container image. `LocalSubprocessSandbox` remains every agent's own default; `DockerSandbox` is real, now genuinely proven, and still unreachable from any real run today. Hardening the sandbox was correctly prioritized over an HTTP route or human-approval gating precisely because the pipeline genuinely runs LLM-generated code — that risk remains fully unmitigated until something actually constructs `DockerSandbox` in place of the local backend, and there is no longer a "does it even work" question standing between here and doing so.

**Recommended: resolve the portable-interpreter gap and wire `DockerSandbox` in as the pipeline's real sandbox backend — closing the loop this step's own prioritization opened.** Concretely, the smallest real slice: decide and implement one portable way for the pack's own agents to invoke a generated file without depending on `sys.executable` (the leading candidate: change `BuildAgentEntrypoint`/`TestAgentEntrypoint`/`DocumentationAgentEntrypoint`'s own write/run scripts to invoke `python3` resolved via the sandbox's own `PATH`, rather than the host's own absolute interpreter path — a small, mechanical change, not a redesign, since every one of this pack's own generated files is already Python by construction), then construct `DockerSandbox` instead of `LocalSubprocessSandbox` at the one place agents currently default it (each entrypoint's own `sandbox or LocalSubprocessSandbox()` line, or a single composition-root-level default the pack's own `pipeline.py` supplies to every agent it constructs). Prove it by re-running this pack's own existing deterministic test suites against the swapped-in backend (no new tests needed if this is done as a parametrized substitution — the same passing/failing outcomes should hold, this time inside a real, network-isolated, filesystem-contained container) plus, once Docker Desktop is available, the two currently-unexecuted live suites (`test_docker_sandbox_live.py`, `test_delivery_pipeline.py`'s own live tier) together for the first genuinely hardened, fully live, end-to-end run this project will have produced.

This is justified over returning to the HTTP route or human-approval gating (both still real, still available candidates):
- Neither reduces the concrete risk this whole hardening step exists to close. An HTTP route would make an *unhardened* pipeline reachable by more callers; human-approval gating adds oversight to *what* happens at named checkpoints without changing *how safely* the sandboxed execution underneath any of them actually runs. Both are genuinely valuable, but neither is the step that makes `DockerSandbox`'s own work matter in practice.
- It is the more honest reading of "done": a sandbox backend that exists, passes its own tests, and is never actually constructed by anything real is exactly the kind of "real but not yet exercised end to end" position this document has flagged for other components before (`SqlKnowledgeWriter`, `SqlKeywordSearcher`) — worth closing quickly rather than letting accumulate.
- It is small and well-bounded: one interpreter-invocation convention change plus one composition-site substitution, not new architecture.

This step would need its own explicit scope fence before implementation (at minimum: the exact form of the portable-interpreter fix, whether `DockerSandbox` becomes the unconditional default or an explicit opt-in the composition root chooses per environment, whether the pipeline's own deterministic tests should run against `DockerSandbox` too or remain `LocalSubprocessSandbox`-only for speed, and explicit confirmation this does not extend to solving dependency installation inside the now-network-isolated container, which ADR-0016 itself assigns to "a separate, explicitly-declared step through an egress proxy" — a distinct, later increment) — consistent with how every minimal-slice step in this document has been separately scoped and approved.

If a different step is preferred instead: **an HTTP route** — extend `kernel/routes/workflows.py`'s own pattern (or add a small, pack-specific route) to accept a `POST` triggering `ai_os_pack_software_engineering.pipeline.build_pipeline_trigger()`, gated by a `workflow:start`-equivalent permission; **human-approval gating** — a real `approvals` table writer (schema already exists, data_model.md §4.5) plus a real `HumanApprovalPoint`-type step executor, most naturally inserted between `test` and `documentation`; a general, Kernel-level "restrict a resolver to specific steps" wrapper, promoting this pack's own pipeline-local `_StepScopedResolver` (§5); digest-pinning the default sandbox image (§5); or the automated manifest -> catalog installer for agents/prompts/workflows alike.

---

## 7. Maintenance

Update this document at the end of every implementation step: move completed items from §4 into §3, refresh §4's gap table, and re-state §6 with the next recommended step.
